# Moore, Russell and Early Wittgenstein — Complete Learning Session

> **Syllabus, verbatim:** *Moore, Russell and Early Wittgenstein : Defence of Commonsense; Refutation of Idealism; Logical Atomism; Logical Constructions; Incomplete Symbols; Picture Theory of Meaning; Sying and Showing* — UPSC Philosophy Paper I, Section A, item 6. The official paper prints **"Sying"**; that printed wording is retained when the clause is quoted, and the correct doctrine-name **Saying and Showing** is used everywhere else.
> **Evidence discipline:** ✅ marks doctrine on which the standard sources converge, ⚠️ marks analytical reconstruction for exam use, and ❓ marks a live scholarly dispute or a contested attribution. Moore and Russell are cited by **paper and year**; the *Tractatus* is cited by **decimal proposition number**, because the numbering is Wittgenstein's own and is identical in every edition and translation, whereas page numbers and wordings are not. Model answers are independent learner practice and are never official UPSC keys.
> **Terminology rule:** the English concept leads and the source term follows immediately in parentheses — *to be is to be perceived (esse est percipi)*, *state of affairs (Sachverhalt)*, *sense (Sinn)*, *senseless (sinnlos)* as against *nonsensical (unsinnig)*, *picture or model (Bild)* and *to depict (abbilden)*, *pictorial form (Form der Abbildung)*, *logical form (logische Form)*, *logical space (logischer Raum)*, *fundamental idea (Grundgedanke)*, *this is how things stand (Es verhält sich so und so)*, *said (gesagt)* as against *shown (gezeigt)*. Moore and Russell wrote in English, so their sentences are quotable as written; every English sentence attributed to Wittgenstein is a **translation**, and where Ogden/Ramsey and Pears–McGuinness diverge the divergence is stated rather than hidden.

## BASIC LEARNING SESSION

### CONCEPT SPINE — ENGLISH FIRST, SOURCE TERM IMMEDIATELY AFTER


*Concept spine: read it downward. One inherited target, three scalpels, one shared insight used three different ways, and three different endings.*

> 🔑 **Mnemonic — the ownership rule, "Moore DEFENDS, Russell DISSECTS, Wittgenstein PICTURES."** Nine of the ten commonest errors in this topic are attribution errors. Fix the owner before you write the doctrine.
> 🔑 **Mnemonic — Russell's descriptions in three beats, "E-U-P".** **E**xistence → **U**niqueness → **P**redication. Write the three conjuncts, then the scope point, then the puzzle solved.
> 🔑 **Mnemonic — Moore's proof in three tests, "D-K-F".** **D**ifferent premiss from conclusion → premiss **K**nown, not merely believed → conclusion **F**ollows.
> 🔑 **Mnemonic — the *Tractatus* ladder of sense, "B-L-G".** **B**ipolarity (the condition of sense) → **L**ogical space (where the possibilities live) → **G**eneral form of the proposition (the rule that generates all of them).
> 🔑 **Mnemonic — the two failure-words, "S before U".** ***Sinnlos*** = senseless but well-formed (tautology, contradiction). ***Unsinnig*** = nonsensical, no sense at all (metaphysical pseudo-proposition). Writing them as synonyms is a marked error.

### ANSWER-WORTHINESS — WHAT EARNS MARKS AND WHAT DOES NOT

| Classification | Content |
|---|---|
| **CORE MAINS** | Moore's list of truisms and the knowing/analysing distinction (1925); the comparative-certainty method and the reversal of the burden of proof; the two-hands proof and Moore's own three conditions for a rigorous proof (1939); the awareness/object distinction and the transparency or diaphanous thesis, with the blue–green–consciousness sentence (1903); Russell's incomplete symbol and the contextual analysis of definite descriptions into existence, uniqueness and predication ("On Denoting", 1905); the three puzzles — negative existentials, excluded middle, substitutivity in attitude contexts; primary and secondary occurrence; the acquaintance/description distinction and the Principle of Acquaintance; logical constructions and the supreme maxim (1914); atomic and molecular propositions, atomic facts, the isomorphism thesis and negative facts (1918); "logical atoms, not physical atoms"; the *Tractatus* on facts rather than things (1.1), objects and states of affairs, the picture theory with pictorial form against logical form, bipolarity, logical space, the general form of the proposition at 6, *sinnlos* against *unsinnig*, saying against showing (4.1212), the ladder (6.54) and proposition 7 |
| **CORE SUPPORTING** | The provenance discipline: three different Moore papers of 1903, 1925 and 1939 with three different arguments; Russell's 1918 preface crediting the ideas to Wittgenstein; the German publication of 1921 and the bilingual *Tractatus* of 1922, with the Latin title suggested by Moore; the Ogden/Ramsey and Pears–McGuinness divergence at proposition 7 and at 4.5 and 6.54; *Sachverhalt* rendered "atomic fact" by Ogden and "state of affairs" by Pears–McGuinness; the fact that there is no proposition 7.1; the N-operator as joint negation; the phase-dependence of Russell's views, so that sense-data acquaintance, the 1918 atomism and later neutral monism are not one timeless doctrine; Strawson's presupposition objection (1950), Sellars on the Given (1956) and Kripke on rigid designation (lectures 1970, book 1980) as one-objection enrichment |
| **SUPPORTING** | Moore's paradox of analysis as method-context for why analysis is not trivial verbal substitution; the colour-exclusion problem of 1929 as a diagnosed internal pressure on the independence of elementary propositions; solipsism and the metaphysical subject at the limit of the world, kept to the depth the say/show boundary requires; the standard-ineffability against resolute/therapeutic dispute over 6.54, stated in one clause and then ruled on |
| **OPTIONAL ADVANCED** | Whether the theory of descriptions is a quantificational device or a deeper account of how apparent names disappear; whether logical constructions are genuine phenomenalist reductions or methodological housekeeping; what "showing" finally means, and whether the *Tractatus* self-destructs; Russell's paradox and the theory of types as the background technique behind "no meaning in isolation"; Moore's naturalistic fallacy named only to be excluded |
| **NOT OWNED HERE** | The verification principle and the rejection of metaphysics as a positive doctrine (Topic 07); meaning as use, language-games, family resemblance and the private-language argument (Topic 08); Strawson's own theory of reference and persons (Topic 11); Berkeley's immaterialism as doctrine (Topic 03); Hegel's Absolute Idealism as doctrine (Topic 05); Moore's ethics, including the naturalistic fallacy and the Open Question Argument; Russell's logicism, *Principia* technique and later neutral monism as systems |

⚠️ **Ownership boundary, stated once.** The printed clause names **three thinkers and seven doctrines**, and it is the only Western clause that makes attribution itself examinable. Everything in this package earns its place because a printed limb or one of the **fourteen** routed 2018–2025 question-parts requires it. Two consequences follow, and both are worth marks. First, no doctrine may be transferred between the three men: Moore's method is comparative certainty, Russell's is quantificational analysis, and early Wittgenstein's is a transcendental account of the conditions of representation, and a script that writes "Moore and Russell held that…" without a discriminating clause has already lost the attribution mark. Second, the topic ends where the *Tractatus* ends: the moment a script writes "and therefore only empirically verifiable statements are meaningful", it has answered Topic 07's question inside Topic 06's answer.

### ORIGIN AND CHRONOLOGY — THE MINIMUM NEEDED TO AVOID ANACHRONISM

⚠️ This clause is a **movement with a datable beginning**, and examiners test the dates through the papers rather than through biography. The rail below is the minimum that prevents the four commonest anachronisms: merging Moore's three papers, dating the English *Tractatus* to 1921, letting Russell's 1918 atomism absorb his later neutral monism, and importing the Vienna Circle into a Tractarian answer.

```text
   THE TARGET ALREADY IN PLACE
   1893  BRADLEY, Appearance and Reality - British Absolute Idealism at
         its height: internal relations, degrees of truth, the ordinary
         world demoted to "appearance"
        |
   THE REVOLT BEGINS - MOORE FIRST, RUSSELL FOLLOWING
   1903  MOORE, "The Refutation of Idealism" (Mind) ......... ACT/OBJECT
   1903  Moore, Principia Ethica - SAME YEAR, DIFFERENT SUBJECT.
         The naturalistic fallacy lives HERE, in ethics, not in item 6
   1905  RUSSELL, "On Denoting" (Mind) .............. DESCRIPTIONS BEGIN
         Ramsey later calls it "a paradigm of philosophy"
   1910-11 Russell, "Knowledge by Acquaintance and Knowledge by
         Description" (Proceedings of the Aristotelian Society)
   1910-13 Whitehead and Russell, Principia Mathematica - the technique
         of CONTEXTUAL DEFINITION that "incomplete symbol" names
   1912  Russell, The Problems of Philosophy, Ch. 5 - the PRINCIPLE OF
         ACQUAINTANCE, safe to quote verbatim
   1914  Russell, Our Knowledge of the External World + "The Relation of
         Sense-Data to Physics" .................. LOGICAL CONSTRUCTIONS
        |
   THE TWO ATOMISMS, WRITTEN IN THE SAME YEARS
   1918  RUSSELL, "The Philosophy of Logical Atomism" - lectures given in

         credits the ideas to Wittgenstein. SAY SO IN THE EXAM.
   1918  WITTGENSTEIN completes the Tractatus manuscript while on active
         service and then in captivity
   1921  German text published as Logisch-Philosophische Abhandlung in
         Annalen der Naturphilosophie
   1922  BILINGUAL EDITION, German-English, with the Latin title
         Tractatus Logico-Philosophicus - the title was MOORE'S
         SUGGESTION. Ogden translation, assisted by Ramsey, checked by
         Wittgenstein. NEVER date the English text 1921.
        |
   MOORE COMPLETES HIS CASE
   1925  MOORE, "A Defence of Common Sense" (Contemporary British
         Philosophy) ................................. THE TRUISMS PAPER
   1939  MOORE, "Proof of an External World" (British Academy lecture)
         ................................................. THE HANDS PAPER
        |
   THE PRESSURES THAT END THE PERIOD
   1929  Wittgenstein, "Some Remarks on Logical Form" (PAS Supplementary)
         - the COLOUR-EXCLUSION problem; the only philosophical article
         besides the Tractatus published in his lifetime, and an analysis
         he quickly abandoned
   1929+ VIENNA CIRCLE takes Tractarian resources in a direction
         Wittgenstein did not take them ................... TOPIC 07
   1950  STRAWSON, "On Referring" (Mind) - presupposition, not falsity
   1953  Philosophical Investigations, posthumous ......... TOPIC 08
   1956  SELLARS, "Empiricism and the Philosophy of Mind" - the Myth of
         the Given, against acquaintance as bedrock
   1961  PEARS-McGUINNESS translation of the Tractatus - divergent
         wording at exactly the most quoted propositions
   1970/1980 KRIPKE, Naming and Necessity - lectures 1970, book 1980;
         give ONE date, not a merged one
```

⚠️ **What the rail earns you.** Three separate marks are commonly available from it: the 1903/1925/1939 separation in any Moore answer; Russell's own 1918 attribution of atomism to Wittgenstein in any atomism answer; and the 1921/1922 distinction in any *Tractatus* answer. None of them costs more than a clause.

### SESSION 1 — THE ANALYTIC REVOLT: THREE PROGRAMMES, ONE TARGET, AND THE ATTRIBUTION FIREWALL

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** At the turn of the twentieth century three Cambridge philosophers stopped trying to build a picture of reality as a whole and started examining, one at a time, the individual sentences in which philosophical claims are made. They asked what a sentence commits you to, what its real structure is, and whether it succeeds in saying anything at all. That change of unit — from *system* to *proposition* — is what is meant by the analytic revolt.

**Technical definition:** **Analytic philosophy**, in its founding phase, is the programme of settling philosophical questions by the **analysis of propositions** rather than by the construction of a metaphysical system. Its shared negative target is **British Absolute Idealism** — the monistic doctrine, associated with F. H. Bradley and J. M. E. McTaggart and inherited ultimately from Hegel, that relations are internal, that ordinary things and ordinary judgements are "appearance", and that only the whole is fully real. Its shared positive commitment is that **grammatical form need not be logical form**. ⚠️ Beyond that shared commitment the three programmes diverge completely in method, in what analysis is supposed to reach, and in what counts as success.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The founders of analytic philosophy were united by what they refused rather than by what they proposed: Moore, Russell and the early Wittgenstein all denied that a philosophical system may overrule what we ordinarily know or ordinarily say, and each then built a completely different instrument for showing why — comparative certainty, quantificational analysis, and a transcendental account of the conditions of sense.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **analysis** — the shared method, and the term whose three different senses this clause turns on
- **British Absolute Idealism** — the named target, not "idealism" in general
- **grammatical form is not logical form** — the single shared insight of Russell and the *Tractatus*
- **act/object distinction** — Moore's instrument, taken over by Russell for sense-data
- **atomic fact / state of affairs (*Sachverhalt*)** — the two atomisms, deliberately kept apart
- **attribution firewall** — the discipline of never letting one man's doctrine drift onto another

**How to use them:** name the target in your first clause, because "revolt" without a named opponent reads as decoration; then state the shared insight; then immediately divide the three programmes, because the division is the marks-bearing content of any comparative question in this clause.

#### Plain-language visual — one enemy, three weapons

```text
   THE THING THEY ALL PUSH AGAINST
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | ABSOLUTE IDEALISM: "Your ordinary judgements are appearance.   |
   | Only the whole is fully real. Relations are internal, so       |
   | nothing is truly separate from anything else."                 |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
                |                |                    |
               v              v                   v
   +---------------+  +--------------------+  +----------------------+
   | MOORE'S       |  | RUSSELL'S          |  | WITTGENSTEIN'S       |
   | WEAPON        |  | WEAPON             |  | WEAPON               |
   | compare the   |  | rewrite the        |  | ask what a           |
   | CERTAINTIES   |  | sentence in        |  | proposition must BE  |
   |               |  | LOGICAL notation   |  | in order to have     |
   | "Your premiss |  |                    |  | sense at all         |
   |  is less      |  | "Your 'the F'      |  |                      |
   |  certain than |  |  was never a name. |  | "Your sentence never |
   |  my hand."    |  |  Watch it vanish." |  |  pictured anything." |
   +---------------+  +--------------------+  +----------------------+
                |                |                    |
               v              v                   v
      knowledge is         ontology is           the boundary of
      protected            slimmed               sense is drawn
```

**In simple words:** imagine three people confronting the same over-confident theory. The first says, *whatever your argument is, I am more sure of my hands than I am of your premises.* The second says, *your sentence looks as though it names something; write it out properly and the apparent name disappears.* The third says, *before we ask whether your sentence is true, ask whether it depicts any possible situation — if it does not, it is not false, it is nonsense.* Those three replies are not variants of one another. They are three separate philosophies that happen to share an enemy.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| The target is a **named** doctrine | British Absolute Idealism, Bradley and McTaggart, not idealism in the abstract |
| The shared insight is about **form** | surface grammar can misdescribe logical structure |
| "Analysis" is **three** methods, not one | commitment-analysis · quantificational analysis · conditions-of-sense analysis |
| Moore is **first** in time | 1903 precedes "On Denoting" (1905) and the *Tractatus* (1918/1921) |
| The influence runs **both ways** | Russell's 1918 preface credits logical atomism to Wittgenstein |

> **One-line memory:** one enemy, three weapons, and the marks are in telling the weapons apart.

⚠️ **Caption.** This table is the single highest-yield object in the topic, because the examiner can test it in a one-line Prelims-style discrimination or make it the assessment half of a 15-mark answer. Learn it as a table, not as prose.

#### 1. What exactly was being revolted against

✅ British Absolute Idealism held that reality is a single spiritual whole; that relations are **internal**, so that nothing could be what it is if its relations were different; and that ordinary claims about separate things in space and time are therefore true only in a degraded sense — "appearance", in Bradley's word, arranged by **degrees of truth**.

⚠️ The consequence that provoked the revolt is a methodological one. If ordinary claims are appearance, then a sufficiently powerful system may correct them wholesale. Moore's answer is that this gets the order of confidence backwards; Russell's is that the doctrine of internal relations is an artefact of taking every proposition to have subject–predicate form; Wittgenstein's is that the system's own sentences fail the test any sentence must pass to have sense.

⚠️ **What not to overwrite.** Hegel's Absolute Idealism is Topic 05 and Berkeley's immaterialism is Topic 03. This session needs the target only as a target. ❌ It is a marked error to answer "Moore's refutation of idealism" by expounding Hegel, and ❌ equally an error to treat Bradley's monism and Berkeley's *esse est percipi* as the same doctrine — Moore's 1903 paper attacks the second, not the first.

#### 2. Three senses of "analysis" — the distinction that most scripts miss

| Programme | What analysis takes as input | What it delivers | When it has succeeded |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Moore** ✅ | an ordinary judgement we already know to be true | an account of what that judgement commits us to | when the commitments are clear; ⚠️ and if they are not, the *knowledge* is untouched |
| **Russell** ✅ | a sentence whose surface suggests a suspect entity | a quantified paraphrase in which the apparent name disappears | when the paraphrase preserves truth-conditions and removes the ontological commitment |
| **Early Wittgenstein** ✅ | any proposition whatever | its resolution into truth-functions of elementary propositions | when the analysis reaches a base of logically independent elementary propositions — ⚠️ a base the *Tractatus* requires but never exhibits |

- ⚠️ **The examinable consequence.** Because "analysis" means three different things, the three men can share a slogan and still disagree about everything that matters. Russell's descriptions do not vindicate Moore's truisms; Moore never accepted that ordinary objects are constructions; Wittgenstein's objects are not Russell's sense-data.

#### 3. Where influence really ran — and why the direction is worth a mark

✅ Russell's 1918 lectures open by disclaiming originality: he presents logical atomism as growing out of conversations with Wittgenstein, his student from 1911. ⚠️ Writing "Russell invented logical atomism and Wittgenstein developed it" is therefore doubly wrong — it reverses the credit Russell himself gave, and it suggests a single doctrine where there are two.

✅ In the other direction the debts are equally concrete: Wittgenstein takes the technique of the **variable and the quantifier** from Russell and Frege, and then removes what he regards as their central mistake — the treatment of logical constants as names of anything (4.0312, the *fundamental idea* or *Grundgedanke*). ✅ Moore's contribution is earlier and different in kind: the **act/object distinction** of 1903, which Russell adopts wholesale for sense-data, and the tone of the whole movement — the refusal to be argued out of what one knows.

#### 4. What each programme finally achieved, stated without inflation

1. **Moore** ✅ reversed the burden of proof in epistemology and made ordinary knowledge a fixed point against which theories are tested. ⚠️ He did not supply a neutral criterion for deciding whose premisses are more certain, and the objection that his proof begs the question against the sceptic is not silenced.
2. **Russell** ✅ produced in the theory of descriptions a technique still in universal use, and with it a general method of ontological economy. ⚠️ He never fixed the final inventory of simples, admitted negative facts as a cost, and changed his own view of the base more than once.
3. **Early Wittgenstein** ✅ gave the most complete account ever attempted of the conditions under which a sentence has sense, and drew from it a boundary around the sayable. ⚠️ The account's own statement falls outside that boundary, which is the ladder problem of 6.54 — a difficulty the book itself raises rather than one imported by critics.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** The early analytic movement is best characterised negatively: what unites Moore, Russell and the early Wittgenstein is a refusal to let a philosophical system overrule ordinary knowledge or ordinary discourse, not a shared positive doctrine.
> **Named evidence.** Moore's "The Refutation of Idealism" (1903) and "A Defence of Common Sense" (1925) protect ordinary certainties by comparing them with sceptical premisses; Russell's "On Denoting" (1905) rewrites "the F is G" as an existentially quantified sentence so that the apparent name disappears; the *Tractatus* (1921/1922) grounds sense in shared logical form and consigns what cannot be depicted to the domain of what can only be shown.
> **Analysis.** Each instrument works on the proposition rather than on the system, which is why the shared insight is formal — that grammatical form may misdescribe logical form — and why the shared enemy is a monism that had made ordinary judgement provisional.
> **Qualification.** The unity is genuine but thin: "analysis" names commitment-clarification in Moore, quantificational paraphrase in Russell and the resolution of propositions into a truth-functional base in Wittgenstein, so a script that treats the three as one programme has recorded the movement's slogan while missing its content.

#### Exam link

- **2018 Q4(c)** and **2024 Q3(a)** both ask how a Russellian technique *leads to* atomism; §2 supplies the discrimination that keeps the answer from sliding into Wittgenstein's atomism.
- **2018 Q3(a)** (Empiricism-owned) explicitly asks whether Moore's reaction to Berkeley differs from Russell's; §2 and §3 are the whole answer to the comparative half.
- **2022 Q1(b)** ("Russell's method of logical analysis") is opened correctly by the third column of §2 and closed by §4.2.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| "Moore, Russell and Wittgenstein held the same view" | They shared a target and an insight about form. Their methods, their ontologies and their criteria of success differ |
| Treating the target as Hegel or Berkeley indifferently | The movement's local opponent is **British Absolute Idealism**; Moore's 1903 paper attacks *esse est percipi* specifically |
| "Russell invented logical atomism" | His own 1918 preface credits the ideas to Wittgenstein — and the two atomisms are still not identical |
| Importing the naturalistic fallacy into this clause | It belongs to *Principia Ethica* (1903) and to ethics. Same author, same year, different syllabus item |
| Ending the topic with verificationism | The *Tractatus* asks whether a sentence depicts a possible situation, not whether it can be empirically checked |

#### Revision notes

- Target: British Absolute Idealism — internal relations, degrees of truth, ordinary world as appearance (Bradley, McTaggart).
- Shared insight: grammatical form is not logical form. Shared unit: the proposition.
- Three analyses: commitment (Moore) · quantificational paraphrase (Russell) · resolution into a truth-functional base (Wittgenstein).
- Ownership: Moore owns commonsense and refutation of idealism; Russell owns atomism, constructions, incomplete symbols; Wittgenstein owns picture theory and saying/showing; atomism is jointly listed and doubly meant.
- Dates for the mark: 1903 Moore · 1905 "On Denoting" · 1914 constructions · 1918 both atomisms · 1921 German, 1922 bilingual · 1925 truisms · 1939 hands.
- Achievements with their costs: burden reversed without a neutral criterion · economy without a fixed inventory · a boundary of sense that its own statement crosses.

#### CLOSING RECALL FLOW — THE REVOLT

```text
BRITISH ABSOLUTE IDEALISM (internal relations, appearance, degrees of truth)
        |
        v
ONE NEGATIVE THESIS: no system may overrule what we ordinarily know or say
        |
        +--> MOORE 1903/1925/1939  compare CERTAINTIES; act != object
        +--> RUSSELL 1905/1914/1918  rewrite LOGICAL FORM; economise ontology
        +--> WITTGENSTEIN 1918/1921/1922  fix the CONDITIONS OF SENSE
        |
        v
SHARED INSIGHT: GRAMMATICAL FORM != LOGICAL FORM
SHARED UNIT:    the PROPOSITION, not the system
        |
        v
ATTRIBUTION FIREWALL: seven printed terms, three owners, one jointly listed
NOT HERE: naturalistic fallacy | verification | meaning-as-use | Strawson's
   positive semantics
        |
        v
ANSWER LINE: they agree that philosophy proceeds by analysis and disagree
   about what analysis reaches
```

### SESSION 2 — MOORE'S DEFENCE OF COMMON SENSE: TRUISMS, KNOWING AGAINST ANALYSING, AND COMPARATIVE CERTAINTY

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Moore says there is a set of very ordinary claims — that the earth existed for many years before he was born, that he has a body, that there are other people with bodies — which he *knows*, with certainty, to be true. Any philosophical argument that ends by denying one of them must contain a premiss he is less sure of than he is of the claim being denied; so the rational response is to reject the argument, not the claim.

**Technical definition:** Moore's **defence of common sense** ("A Defence of Common Sense", 1925) consists of (i) a list of ordinary propositions — his **truisms** — which he asserts he knows with certainty and which he claims are known by very many others; (ii) the distinction between **knowing that a proposition is true** and **knowing its correct analysis**; and (iii) a **comparative-certainty** principle: a philosophical argument overturns a truism only if every premiss of the argument is more certain than the truism, and no sceptical or idealist premiss has ever met that condition. ⚠️ "Common sense" here is not opinion-polling, convention or majority belief; the list is philosophically contestable rather than infallible by definition.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Moore's defence of common sense is not the claim that ordinary belief is beyond criticism but the far narrower and far more effective claim that no philosopher has ever produced a premiss more certain than "I have a body" — so that when a system and a truism collide, it is the system whose credentials must be re-examined first.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **truisms** — Moore's own word for the propositions on the list
- **knowing that P against knowing the analysis of P** — the distinction that does the defensive work
- **comparative certainty** — the method: weigh the premiss against the proposition it would overturn
- **reversal of the burden of proof** — the result, stated in the examiner's language
- **common-sense realism** — the label the 2019 paper uses; explain *why* it is applied
- ⚠️ **not a popularity claim** — say this explicitly; it pre-empts the commonest objection

**How to use them:** never open a Moore answer with the hands. Open with the *structure* — a list, a distinction and a comparison — because the 2019, 2021 and 2024 questions all ask for arguments, and the hands are a separate paper of 1939 answering a separate question.

#### Plain-language visual — the weighing scale

```text
   THE SCEPTIC'S ARGUMENT              MOORE'S TRUISM
   +---------------------------+       +---------------------------+
   | P1  I cannot rule out     |       |  I have a body.           |
   |     that I am dreaming.   |       |                           |
   | P2  If I cannot rule that |  vs   |  The earth existed for    |
   |     out, I do not know    |       |  many years before I was  |
   |     I have a body.        |       |  born.                    |
   | C   So I do not know I    |       |                           |
   |     have a body.          |       |  There are other human    |
   +---------------------------+       |  beings, with bodies.     |
                                       +---------------------------+
              HOW CERTAIN                        HOW CERTAIN
              ARE P1 AND P2?                     IS THE TRUISM?
                   |                                   |
                  v                                  v
        abstract, technical,                 as certain as anything
        contested by competent               I have ever been
        philosophers
                   |                                   |
                   +----------------> WEIGH <----------+
                                        |
                                        v
                   THE ARGUMENT IS VALID, SO ONE OF THREE THINGS
                   MUST GO. MOORE: GIVE UP A PREMISS, NOT THE
                   CONCLUSION YOU WERE MOST SURE OF.
```

**In simple words:** a valid argument is a set of scales, not a one-way street. If the conclusion is something you are more certain is false than you are certain any premiss is true, then the argument has not proved its conclusion — it has *refuted one of its own premisses*. Moore's contribution is to insist that we actually perform the weighing instead of assuming that the philosophical premiss automatically wins because it is philosophical.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Truisms are **known**, not merely believed | Moore's claim is epistemic, and he insists on the word "know" |
| Knowing is not analysing | difficulty about what "material object" *means* leaves the knowledge standing |
| The method is **comparative** | it never claims the truism is self-evidently unassailable, only that it outweighs its rivals |
| The list is **contestable** | Moore offers it as what he knows, not as a definition of truth |
| The result is **dialectical** | the sceptic now owes an account of why abstract premisses beat perceptual ones |

> **One-line memory:** the argument is valid — so which end of it do you trust more?

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — the structure of the 1925 defence

```text
   STEP 1  THE LIST OF TRUISMS (Moore's own examples)
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | "There exists at present a living human body, which is my      |
   |  body."                                                        |
   | "This body has existed continuously on or near the earth."     |
   | "The earth has existed for many years past."                   |
   | "There have existed many other human beings, with bodies."     |
   | "I am a human being who has had many experiences."             |
   | AND: very many other human beings know propositions of the     |
   |  same kind about themselves.                                   |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
                              |
   STEP 2  THE LEVEL DISTINCTION - the load-bearing move
   +--------------------------------+------------------------------+
   | KNOWING THAT P IS TRUE         | KNOWING THE ANALYSIS OF P    |
   | certain: "I have two hands"    | doubtful: what IS a material |
   |                                | object? sense-data? a class  |
   | pre-philosophical, secure      | of appearances? a substance? |
   |                                | PHILOSOPHICAL WORK PROPER,   |
   |                                | and it may remain unfinished |
   +--------------------------------+------------------------------+
   THE FALLACY MOORE NAMES: "you cannot know you have hands because
   you cannot analyse 'material object'" CONFUSES THE TWO LEVELS.
                              |
   STEP 3  THE COMPARATIVE-CERTAINTY PRINCIPLE
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | For a philosophical argument to overturn a truism, EVERY       |
   | premiss must be MORE certain than the truism it denies.        |
   | Moore's empirical claim: no such premiss has been produced.    |
   | Therefore the rational move is to reject a premiss.            |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
                              |
   STEP 4  THE RESULT, IN THE EXAMINER'S WORDS
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | THE BURDEN OF PROOF IS REVERSED. The realist no longer owes a  |
   | proof of the external world from neutral ground; the sceptic   |
   | owes an explanation of why his abstract premisses should be    |
   | trusted more than an ordinary perceptual certainty.            |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
                              |
   THE LIMIT THAT MUST BE WRITTEN
   ⚠️ The principle presupposes that certainty is COMPARABLE across
   ordinary and philosophical propositions, and Moore supplies NO
   NEUTRAL MEASURE for the comparison. A sceptic who simply denies
   that Moore knows the truism has not been shown to be irrational -
   he has been told that Moore is more confident.
```

⚠️ **Caption.** Steps 1–3 are what the 2021 and 2024 questions call "the arguments". Step 4 is the verdict. The bottom band is the assessment half, and the questions in 2021 and 2024 both explicitly demand it.

#### 1. Why the list matters as a list

✅ Moore does not argue *for* the truisms from something more basic — that would concede that they need support from outside. He **asserts** them, and asserts in addition that he knows them, and that others know propositions of the same kind. The list is therefore doing two jobs at once: it fixes what is to be defended, and it displays how enormous the sceptic's task is, since the sceptic must deny not one claim but a whole interlocking body of them.

⚠️ **The second-order claim is the more interesting one.** Moore also claims to know that *other people know* such propositions. This matters because it converts the defence from a report of personal confidence into a claim about a shared epistemic position, and because it is what makes "common sense" the right name — the propositions are common to us, not merely evident to Moore.

❌ **What the doctrine is not.** It is not the claim that whatever most people believe is true; it is not an appeal to convention; and it is not a claim of infallibility. Moore's own list is a philosophical proposal and can be argued with. State this in one clause and the commonest objection is disarmed before it is raised.

#### 2. The knowing/analysing distinction, and the confusion of levels it exposes

| The sceptical or idealist move | Moore's diagnosis |
|---|---|
| "The analysis of 'material object' is disputed, therefore we do not know that material objects exist" ✅ | A **confusion of levels**: the truth of a proposition and the correct account of its constituents are different questions, established by different means |
| "Since sense-data are all we are directly given, ordinary claims are at best inferences" ⚠️ | Moore accepts a real question about how truisms relate to sense-data; he denies that leaving it open suspends the knowledge |
| "Ordinary language is loose, so ordinary claims are imprecise" ⚠️ | Imprecision in stating a proposition is not doubt about it; Moore's certainty attaches to the proposition, not to a formulation |

- ✅ **Why this is the load-bearing move.** Without it, the defence would be a bare refusal to engage. With it, Moore can concede the entire difficulty of philosophical analysis — he was himself a lifelong analyst, and the paradox of analysis is *his* problem — while denying that the difficulty reaches the knowledge.
- ⚠️ **The honest cost.** The distinction protects the knowledge only if propositional knowledge really can be secure while its constituents are unclear. That is exactly what a sceptic about the given, or a philosopher who ties understanding to analysis, will deny.

#### 3. The paradox of analysis — the difficulty Moore's own method creates ⚠️

If the correct analysis of "X is a brother" is "X is a male sibling", then either the analysis says the very same thing as the original, in which case it is trivially uninformative; or it says something different, in which case it is not an analysis of that concept at all.

- ⚠️ The paradox is associated with Moore and has **no settled Moorean solution**. Fregean differences of sense or mode of presentation, and later accounts of concept-possession and synonymy, are proposed responses rather than agreed ones.
- ⚠️ **Owner scope.** This is **method-context, not a printed limb**. Use it for one purpose only: to show why analysis cannot be reduced to trivial verbal substitution, and therefore why Moore needed the knowing/analysing distinction in the first place. Do not build a section of an answer on it.

#### 4. Why the label "common-sense realism" is applied — the 2019 question in one paragraph

✅ **Common sense**, because the defended propositions are the ordinary ones and the method is comparison with them rather than derivation from a philosophical first principle. ✅ **Realism**, because the truisms include the existence of a body, of the earth before one's birth and of other human beings — that is, of things whose existence does not consist in being perceived by the person asserting them. ⚠️ **The qualification that earns the assessment mark:** the realism is a *consequence* of defending the truisms, not an independently argued metaphysics. Moore gives no theory of perception, does not settle whether ordinary objects are analysable into sense-data, and offers no account of the relation between the two levels. His realism is the position he ends in, not the premiss he starts from.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** Moore's defence of common sense succeeds as a redistribution of dialectical burden and fails, by design, as a demonstration that scepticism is false.
> **Named evidence.** "A Defence of Common Sense" (1925) lists truisms — that he has a body, that the earth existed for many years before his birth, that there are other human beings with bodies — asserts that he knows them, distinguishes knowing a proposition from knowing its analysis, and rests the defence on the comparison of certainties.
> **Analysis.** Because a valid argument transmits certainty backwards as well as forwards, an argument whose conclusion is less credible than its own premisses are is one that has refuted a premiss; Moore's contribution is to insist that the comparison actually be made, and to point out that no sceptical premiss has ever been more evident than "I have a body".
> **Qualification.** The method presupposes that certainty can be compared across ordinary and philosophical propositions without a neutral measure, so a sceptic who simply denies that Moore *knows* the truism has not been refuted but only outweighed — which is why Moore's own verdict is that scepticism has not been shown to be true rather than that it has been shown to be false.

#### Exam link

- **2019 Q3(c)** ("Why is Moore's philosophy called common-sense realism?") is answered by §4, with §1–§2 as the supporting structure. The question asks *why the label is applied*, so the answer must justify both words.
- **2024 Q1(c)** ("What arguments are offered by Moore to prove that there are certain truisms…? Critically discuss.") takes §1–§3 for the arguments and the bottom band of the visual for the "critically".
- **2021 Q3(a)**, twenty marks, adds the explicit sceptical half; that question is completed in Session 3.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| Answering the 1925 question with the 1939 hands | Different paper, different argument. The truisms paper contains no proof; the proof paper is a separate lecture |
| "Common sense means what most people believe" | It means a specific list of ordinary propositions Moore claims to know; popularity plays no role in the argument |
| Presenting the truisms as self-evident axioms | Moore claims to *know* them, not that they are indubitable by definition; the list is philosophically contestable |
| Omitting the knowing/analysing distinction | Without it the defence looks like a refusal to argue, and the answer loses its central move |
| Claiming Moore refutes scepticism | He shifts the burden. His own conclusion is that no sceptical premiss has been shown more certain than the truism it denies |

#### Revision notes

- 1925 "A Defence of Common Sense": list of truisms + second-order claim that others know propositions of the same kind.
- Knowing that P is not knowing the analysis of P; the sceptic's inference from analytic difficulty to ignorance is a confusion of levels.
- Comparative certainty: an argument overturns a truism only if every premiss is more certain than the truism.
- Result: reversal of the burden of proof; ⚠️ no neutral measure of certainty is supplied.
- ❌ Not popularity, not convention, not infallibility.
- Paradox of analysis: bounded method-context, no settled Moorean solution, explains why analysis is not verbal substitution.
- "Common-sense realism": common sense from the method, realism from the content of the list; the realism is a result, not a premiss.

#### CLOSING RECALL FLOW — THE DEFENCE OF COMMON SENSE

```text
1925 "A DEFENCE OF COMMON SENSE"
        |
        +--> LIST OF TRUISMS (my body | the earth before my birth |
        |    other human beings with bodies) + others know the same kind
        |
        +--> LEVEL DISTINCTION: knowing that P  !=  knowing P's ANALYSIS
        |    so analytic difficulty does NOT cancel knowledge
        |
        +--> COMPARATIVE CERTAINTY: every premiss must outweigh the
             truism it denies - and none ever has
        |
        v
RESULT: THE BURDEN OF PROOF IS REVERSED
        |
        v
LIMIT (write it): no NEUTRAL measure of certainty is supplied; the
   sceptic is outweighed, not shown to be irrational
        |
        v
LABEL: COMMON-SENSE (method) + REALISM (content of the list), and the
   realism is a RESULT, not an independently argued metaphysics
ANSWER LINE: no philosopher has produced a premiss more certain than
   "I have a body"
```

### SESSION 3 — MOORE'S PROOF OF AN EXTERNAL WORLD: THREE CONDITIONS, TWO HANDS, AND THE SCEPTICAL REPLY

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** In a 1939 British Academy lecture Moore claims to give a *strict proof* that things exist outside us. He holds up one hand and says "Here is one hand", holds up the other and says "and here is another", and points out that if that is true then at least two things exist in space — and therefore an external world exists. The point is not the theatricality; it is that Moore then states, in advance, the three conditions a rigorous proof must satisfy, and argues that his gesture satisfies all three.

**Technical definition:** Moore's **proof of an external world** ("Proof of an External World", 1939) is a valid argument from the premiss that two hands are present here and now to the conclusion that at least two **things met with in space** exist, and hence that an external world exists. Its philosophical content lies in Moore's **three conditions for a rigorous proof** — the premiss must be *different from* the conclusion, the premiss must be *known* and not merely believed, and the conclusion must *follow* from the premiss — and in his admission that he cannot in turn **prove the premiss**, coupled with the claim that this admission does not defeat the proof.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Moore's two hands are not a joke at the sceptic's expense but the most economical statement in modern epistemology of a question the sceptic must answer: if a valid argument from a premiss I know delivers the existence of an external world, then the demand for a *further* proof of the premiss is a demand that no proof of anything could ever satisfy.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **things met with in space** — Moore's own gloss on "external"; quote it, because it is what makes the inference valid
- **three conditions for a rigorous proof** — different premiss · known premiss · valid consequence
- **proof against proving the premiss** — the distinction Moore explicitly concedes
- **question-begging objection** — the strongest reply, which must be stated
- **hinge or framework proposition** ⚠️ — the later diagnosis, named as a comparison and *not* as Moore's own view
- **right hand first, then the left** ⚠️ — the printed order, if you narrate the gesture at all

**How to use them:** the marks in this session are in the *conditions*, not the gesture. Most scripts describe the hands and stop; the strong script writes the three conditions, checks each, and then concedes exactly what Moore concedes.

#### Plain-language visual — what a proof is allowed to look like

```text
   A PROOF IS RIGOROUS WHEN THREE THINGS ARE TRUE OF IT
   =====================================================================
   TEST 1  THE PREMISS IS NOT THE CONCLUSION IN DISGUISE
           "Here is a hand"  !=  "An external world exists"
           -> so the argument is NOT CIRCULAR
   ---------------------------------------------------------------------
   TEST 2  THE PREMISS IS KNOWN, NOT MERELY BELIEVED OR ASSUMED
           Moore: "I did know it. It would have been absurd to suggest
           that I did not know it, but only believed it."
           -> so the argument has a GENUINE STARTING POINT
   ---------------------------------------------------------------------
   TEST 3  THE CONCLUSION REALLY FOLLOWS
           A hand is a THING MET WITH IN SPACE.
           "External" just means: met with in space, not dependent on my
           being conscious of it.
           -> so the inference is VALID, not a leap
   =====================================================================
   AND ONE THING MOORE ADMITS HE CANNOT DO
           He cannot PROVE the premiss "Here is one hand".
           To do that he would have to prove he was not dreaming -
           "and that I cannot do."
   =====================================================================
   THE QUESTION THAT DECIDES THE ANSWER
           Is a proof worthless because its premiss is unproved?
           MOORE: NO. Otherwise nothing whatever could be proved,
           because every proof must start somewhere.
           SCEPTIC: YES, HERE. Because this premiss is precisely what
           was in dispute.
```

**In simple words:** everyone agrees you cannot prove everything — some starting point must be known without being derived from something else. The whole question is whether "here is a hand" is allowed to be such a starting point. Moore says it obviously is, because it is about as good a case of knowing as anyone can produce. The sceptic says it obviously is not, because it is exactly the sort of claim his argument targeted. Neither side is being stupid; the disagreement is about where the burden lies, and that is the honest verdict to write.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| The paper is **1939**, and separate | the truisms paper is 1925; do not merge them |
| "External" is **defined**, not assumed | things met with in space — this is what makes the inference valid |
| The three conditions are Moore's own | he states them before giving the proof, which is why the proof is not naïve |
| He **concedes** he cannot prove the premiss | and argues that this does not disqualify the proof |
| The gesture order is **right, then left** | ⚠️ narrate it correctly or omit the detail |

> **One-line memory:** a proof needs a *known* starting point, not a *proved* one.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — the proof, the objection and the two ways of answering it

```text
   THE ARGUMENT
   1  Here is one hand.                        (present perceptual
   2  And here is another.                      knowledge)
   3  So at least two material things exist.   (hands are things met
   4  So an external world exists.              with in space)
        |
        v
   THE SCEPTIC'S REPLY - and it is a serious one
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | "Premiss 1 is exactly what I put in doubt. If dreaming or      |
   |  deception cannot be excluded, you do not KNOW premiss 1, and  |
   |  test 2 fails. Your proof therefore BEGS THE QUESTION."        |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
        |
        +----------------------------+----------------------------+
        |                            |                            |
       v                          v                           v
   MOORE'S OWN REPLY          THE DIALECTICAL         THE LATER, DIFFERENT
   (1939)                     DEFENCE (Pryor and      DIAGNOSIS (On
   I need not prove every     others) ⚠️              Certainty) ⚠️❓
   premiss I know. And no     Moore need not          Perhaps such
   sceptical premiss is       CONVINCE the sceptic;   propositions are not
   more certain than the      he needs to show that   ordinary knowledge-
   ordinary proposition it    the sceptic's premisses claims at all but
   is used to deny.           are the weaker part of  FRAMEWORK or HINGE
                              the argument.           certainties on which
                                                      doubt itself depends.
        |                            |                            |
       v                          v                           v
   VERDICT SPINE: the proof is FORMALLY VALID; it does not supply a
   NEUTRAL criterion of knowledge; and its force is comparative, which
   is precisely what Moore claimed for it and nothing more.
   ⚠️ OWNER NOTE: the hinge/framework diagnosis belongs to LATER
   WITTGENSTEIN (Topic 08). Name it as a comparison in one clause;
   do not expound On Certainty inside a Moore answer.
```

⚠️ **Caption.** The three columns are three *different* defences, and only the first is Moore's own. Marking the difference is what converts a description of the proof into an assessment of it.

#### 1. What Moore is actually proving, and against whom

✅ The lecture's target is not the general sceptic first of all but **Kant**, who had called it "a scandal to philosophy" that no satisfactory proof of the existence of things outside us had been given. Moore takes the challenge literally: he offers a proof, and to make it assessable he first defines "external to our minds" as **"things met with in space"** — things whose existence does not consist in being experienced.

⚠️ **Why the definition is doing work.** Without it, the step from "here is a hand" to "an external world exists" would be an unexplained leap. With it, the step is analytic: a hand is a thing met with in space; an external thing just *is* a thing met with in space; so if there is a hand there is an external thing. ❌ A script that gives the gesture and omits the gloss has left the inference unjustified and will be marked down for it.

#### 2. The three conditions, and why Moore states them first

| Condition ✅ | What it excludes | Does the hands proof meet it? |
|---|---|---|
| The premiss must be **different from** the conclusion | circular arguments in which the conclusion is smuggled into the premiss | Yes — "here is a hand" is not synonymous with "an external world exists" |
| The premiss must be **known**, not merely believed or assumed | arguments from assumptions, however plausible | Moore insists he *knew* it; ❓ this is precisely the contested condition |
| The conclusion must **follow** | non sequiturs and rhetorical gestures | Yes, given the gloss on "external" |

- ✅ **The rhetorical point.** By stating the conditions in advance, Moore removes the option of dismissing the proof as unserious. Any critic must now say *which* condition fails, and the only candidate is the second.
- ⚠️ **The concession, in Moore's own terms.** He grants that he cannot prove the premiss, since doing so would require proving that he is not dreaming, "and that I cannot do". He denies that this is a defect: a proof requires a *known* premiss, not a *proved* one, and the contrary demand generates an infinite regress in which nothing is ever proved.

#### 3. The strongest objection, and the three replies worth writing ❓

**The objection.** The proof begs the question. The sceptic's argument was designed to show that Moore does *not* know the premiss; asserting that he does know it therefore assumes what was in dispute, and condition 2 is unmet.

1. **Moore's own reply** ✅ — the comparative one. He is not obliged to establish the premiss from neutral ground. The relevant comparison is between the certainty of "here is a hand" and the certainty of the sceptic's premisses, and the ordinary proposition wins.
2. **The dialectical reply** ⚠️ — associated with later defenders such as James Pryor. Moore's proof is not addressed to a sceptic who has suspended all perceptual knowledge; it is addressed to us, and it shows that the *cost* of scepticism is the surrender of a piece of knowledge better established than any premiss used to attack it.
3. **The later, and different, diagnosis** ⚠️ ❓ — *On Certainty* re-examines whether propositions like "here is a hand" function as ordinary knowledge-claims at all, or as **framework certainties** that make doubting possible and cannot themselves be sensibly doubted. ⚠️ **Boundary:** this is Wittgenstein's later work and belongs to Topic 08. Name it in a clause as the most important later assessment, and stop.

- ⚠️ **The verdict that a strong script writes.** The proof is valid and its premiss is as well placed as any premiss can be; what it does not supply is a *neutral criterion* by which the comparison of certainties could be adjudicated. Moore's achievement is to have made the sceptic argue for his premisses rather than assume the burden lies elsewhere.

#### 4. How Sessions 2 and 3 combine into the twenty-mark answer

✅ **2021 Q3(a)** asks for the arguments of the 1925 paper *and* whether they answer the sceptic. The correct architecture is therefore:

1. The **list** and the second-order claim (1925).
2. The **knowing/analysing** distinction, with the confusion of levels named.
3. The **comparative-certainty** principle stated as a principle, not an attitude.
4. The **1939 proof** as the same principle in an explicit deductive form, with the three conditions.
5. The **objection** — question-begging — and the three replies.
6. A **graded verdict**: sufficient as a redistribution of burden, insufficient as a refutation, and honest about the missing neutral measure.

⚠️ Note that step 4 is where the two papers meet. They are separate texts, but they execute one strategy: *compare the credentials of the competing claims instead of assuming the philosophical claim wins*.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** Moore's proof of an external world is best read not as an attempt to convert the sceptic but as a demonstration that the sceptic's demand, pressed consistently, would make proof of anything impossible.
> **Named evidence.** In the British Academy lecture of 1939 Moore defines external things as "things met with in space", argues from "Here is one hand, and here is another" to the existence of at least two such things, and states three conditions of rigour — the premiss must differ from the conclusion, must be known rather than believed, and must entail it — while conceding that he cannot prove the premiss itself because he cannot prove he is not dreaming.
> **Analysis.** The concession is not an oversight but the point: every proof terminates in a premiss that is known without being derived, so a demand for proof of *every* premiss is a demand for an infinite regress, and the real question is only whether an ordinary perceptual claim may occupy that terminal position.
> **Qualification.** Because the sceptic's argument was precisely an argument that the premiss is not known, Moore's insistence that he knows it settles the dispute only for someone who already shares his ranking of certainties — which is why the later assessment of these propositions as framework certainties in *On Certainty*, though it belongs to Wittgenstein's later philosophy rather than to Moore, remains the most serious attempt to say what Moore's proof got right.

#### Exam link

- **2021 Q3(a)**, twenty marks, is the direct home of this session; §4 gives the architecture and §3 supplies the "sufficient response to objections presented by the sceptic" half of the question.
- **2019 Q3(c)** ("common-sense realism") uses the proof as the realism half — the hands are the clearest case of a thing met with in space.
- **2024 Q1(c)** should mention the proof only in a closing clause; that question is about the 1925 arguments, and importing the whole 1939 lecture unbalances a ten-mark answer.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| Narrating the hands and omitting the three conditions | The conditions are the argument. Without them the answer is anecdote |
| Omitting "things met with in space" | Then the inference from hands to an external world is an unexplained leap |
| Saying Moore proves the premiss | He explicitly says he cannot, and explains why that is not required |
| Treating *On Certainty* as Moore's own answer | It is later Wittgenstein, Topic 08 — a comparison in one clause, never an expansion |
| Reversing the gesture order | ⚠️ Moore's text specifies the right hand first, then the left. If unsure, write "first one hand and then the other" |

#### Revision notes

- 1939 British Academy lecture; target is Kant's "scandal to philosophy" charge.
- "External" is glossed as **things met with in space**; this gloss makes the inference valid.
- Three conditions: premiss different from conclusion · premiss known, not believed · conclusion follows.
- Moore concedes he cannot *prove* the premiss (he cannot prove he is not dreaming) and denies that this is a defect.
- Objection: begs the question. Replies: comparative certainty (Moore) · dialectical burden-shifting (Pryor) ⚠️ · hinge/framework re-description (*On Certainty*) ⚠️ ❓ Topic 08.
- Verdict: valid proof, no neutral criterion of knowledge, force is comparative — exactly what Moore claimed.
- 1925 + 1939 = one strategy in two texts: compare credentials rather than assume the philosophical premiss wins.

#### CLOSING RECALL FLOW — THE PROOF OF AN EXTERNAL WORLD

```text
KANT'S CHALLENGE: it is "a scandal" that no proof of external things exists
        |
        v
MOORE 1939: define EXTERNAL = THINGS MET WITH IN SPACE
        |
        v
1 here is one hand -> 2 and here is another -> 3 two things met with in
space exist -> 4 an external world exists
        |
        v
THREE CONDITIONS OF RIGOUR
   [1] premiss DIFFERENT from conclusion  -> not circular
   [2] premiss KNOWN, not merely believed -> contested condition
   [3] conclusion FOLLOWS                 -> valid, given the gloss
        |
        v
CONCESSION: cannot PROVE the premiss (cannot prove he is not dreaming)
   -> a proof needs a KNOWN premiss, not a PROVED one, or nothing is
      ever proved
        |
        v
OBJECTION: begs the question
   REPLY 1 comparative certainty (Moore's own)
   REPLY 2 dialectical burden-shift (Pryor) ⚠️
   REPLY 3 hinge/framework certainty (On Certainty) ⚠️ TOPIC 08 ONLY
        |
        v
VERDICT: formally valid | no neutral criterion supplied | comparative
   force is exactly what was claimed
ANSWER LINE: the demand to prove every premiss is a demand that no proof
   could ever meet
```

### SESSION 4 — MOORE'S REFUTATION OF IDEALISM: AWARENESS, OBJECT AND THE TRANSPARENCY THESIS

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Idealists had argued that to exist is to be perceived. Moore replies that every experience has two parts that must be kept apart — the *awareness*, which is the same in every experience, and the *object*, which is what makes one experience different from another. When you are aware of blue and then of green, the awareness is common and the objects differ; so the blue is not the same thing as the awareness of blue. If they are not the same thing, the idealist's identification of being with being-perceived has lost its argument.

**Technical definition:** Moore's **refutation of idealism** ("The Refutation of Idealism", *Mind*, 1903) targets the principle **to be is to be perceived (*esse est percipi*)** as it functions in Berkeleyan idealism and, more broadly, the thesis that reality is mind-dependent. The argument turns on the **act/object distinction**: in every sensation there is (i) an act of awareness or consciousness and (ii) an object of which one is aware. The idealist inference requires these to be *identical* or the object to be a *constituent* of the act. Moore argues that they are distinct, since the awareness is the common element in the sensations of blue and of green while the objects differ. He explains the persistent temptation to conflate them by the **transparency** or **diaphanous** character of consciousness: attention passes through the act to the object, so the act is easily overlooked. ⚠️ The conclusion is deliberately narrow: it blocks one argument *for* idealism; it does not by itself establish that objects exist unperceived.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Moore's refutation of idealism is a single distinction pressed with unusual severity: if what I am aware of were the same thing as my awareness of it, the sensation of blue and the sensation of green would have nothing in common and nothing to differ in — so the very intelligibility of experience requires the act–object distinction that *esse est percipi* must deny.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **to be is to be perceived (*esse est percipi*)** — quote the Latin, then the English gloss
- **act of awareness against object of awareness** — the whole argument in five words
- **the common element and the variable element** — how the blue/green contrast is generated
- **transparency or diaphanous thesis** — why the conflation is so tempting
- **self-contradictory mistake** ⚠️ — Moore's own charge in the 2020 quotation; use his phrase
- **bounded conclusion** — one idealist argument blocked, mind-independence not thereby proved

**How to use them:** in a quotation-led question, name the paper and the year in the first line, then the target, then the distinction. The examiner is checking whether you can locate a sentence in an argument, not whether you can gloss it.

#### Plain-language visual — the same lamp, two slides

```text
   TWO EXPERIENCES
   +-------------------------------+  +-------------------------------+
   | SENSATION OF BLUE             |  | SENSATION OF GREEN            |
   |                               |  |                               |
   |   [ AWARENESS ] --> ( BLUE )  |  |   [ AWARENESS ] --> ( GREEN ) |
   +-------------------------------+  +-------------------------------+
              ^                                    ^
              |                                    |
        SAME in both                        DIFFERENT in each
        = the COMMON element                = the VARIABLE element
              |                                    |
              +----------------+-------------------+
                               |
                               v
        IF awareness WERE identical with its object, then the two
        sensations would share NOTHING (their objects differ) and
        would differ in NOTHING (there is nothing else in them).
        Both consequences are false. THEREFORE they are distinct.
                               |
                               v
        WHY WE KEEP MISSING IT: the lamp analogy
        A projector lamp is what lets you see the slide; you look
        THROUGH it and see the picture. Asked what you saw, you say
        "a blue square" - never "a lamp". Consciousness is like the
        lamp: DIAPHANOUS, easily overlooked, and therefore easily
        collapsed into what it reveals.
```

**In simple words:** if you insist that the seeing and the seen are one and the same thing, then when you look at something blue and then at something green you have not looked at two different things with one faculty — you have simply had two experiences with nothing whatever in common. But they obviously do have something in common: both are cases of *being aware*. That common element cannot be the blue, because the green case has no blue in it. So awareness is one thing and its object is another.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| The paper is **1903**, in *Mind* | not *Principia Ethica*, published the same year |
| The target is ***esse est percipi*** | the Berkeleyan equation, not idealism in every form |
| The argument is **structural** | it uses the intelligibility of comparison, not an appeal to intuition |
| Transparency **explains the error** | it is a diagnosis, not an extra premiss |
| The conclusion is **narrow** | one route to idealism is blocked; mind-independence needs more |

> **One-line memory:** what varies cannot be what stays the same.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — the argument, the diagnosis and the exact boundary of the result

```text
   STEP 1  THE IDEALIST PRINCIPLE, AS MOORE STATES IT
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | esse est percipi - "to be is to be perceived". For the         |
   | inference to work, the object's BEING must be nothing over and |
   | above its BEING PERCEIVED. The sensation and its object must   |
   | therefore be ONE THING, or the object a mere constituent of    |
   | the act.                                                       |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
                              |
   STEP 2  THE DISTINCTION
   +--------------------------------+------------------------------+
   | ACT OF AWARENESS               | OBJECT OF AWARENESS          |
   | consciousness; the same in     | blue, green, this sound;     |
   | every sensation                | different in each sensation  |
   +--------------------------------+------------------------------+
                              |
   STEP 3  THE ARGUMENT FROM COMPARISON
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | "Blue is one object of sensation and green is another, and     |
   |  consciousness, which both sensations have in common, is       |
   |  different from either."                                       |
   |                                                                |
   | If awareness = object, the two sensations share nothing;       |
   | if awareness = object, they differ in nothing further.         |
   | Both are false. Hence awareness != object.                     |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
                              |
   STEP 4  THE 2020 SENTENCE - the same point in Moore's sharper form
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | "If anyone tells us that to say 'Blue exists' is the same      |
   |  thing as to say that 'Both blue and consciousness exist', he  |
   |  makes a mistake and a SELF-CONTRADICTORY mistake."            |
   |                                                                |
   | Why self-contradictory: the speaker asserts that the two       |
   | statements have the SAME content while using a distinction     |
   | between two items - blue and consciousness - to state the      |
   | second. He must have the distinction in order to deny it.      |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
                              |
   STEP 5  THE DIAGNOSIS - transparency / diaphanousness
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | Introspect the sensation of blue and what you find is the      |
   | BLUE. The act is elusive - "as if it were diaphanous". Being   |
   | hard to notice is not the same as not being there; the         |
   | idealist mistakes elusiveness for absence.                     |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
                              |
   STEP 6  THE BOUNDARY - the half of the answer most scripts omit
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | ACHIEVED ✅  the identity route from object to act is blocked, |
   |              so esse est percipi is not established            |
   | NOT ACHIEVED ⚠️ that any object exists UNPERCEIVED             |
   | NOT ACHIEVED ⚠️ a positive theory of perception                |
   | NOT ACHIEVED ⚠️ a refutation of ABSOLUTE idealism, which does  |
   |              not rest on the esse est percipi equation at all  |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
```

⚠️ **Caption.** Steps 3 and 4 are the two quotations the 2025 and 2020 papers print. Step 6 is where the assessment marks are: an answer that claims Moore refutes idealism *tout court* has overclaimed on Moore's behalf.

#### 1. Why the argument is stronger than an appeal to introspection

✅ Moore does not simply report that he can detect two items. He argues that the idealist's own identification makes experience **unintelligible**: the sensations of blue and of green must have something in common, since both are sensations, and must differ in something, since they are of different colours. On the identity thesis there is only one item in each, so there is nothing available to play both roles.

⚠️ This is why the argument survives the standard complaint that consciousness cannot be introspectively isolated. Moore *agrees* that it cannot easily be isolated — that is the transparency thesis — and turns the concession into an explanation of the error rather than an objection to the distinction.

#### 2. The 2020 sentence, decoded ✅

The printed quotation charges the idealist with "a mistake and a **self-contradictory** mistake". The self-contradiction is precise, and stating it exactly is the difference between a competent and a strong answer:

1. The claim is that "Blue exists" and "Both blue and consciousness exist" say the **same thing**.
2. But the second statement is formed by **conjoining two distinct items**, blue and consciousness.
3. To assert the identity of content, the speaker must therefore already **possess and use** the very distinction whose significance he is denying.
4. Therefore the identification is not merely false; it is unstatable without presupposing its own falsity.

⚠️ **The residual.** The self-contradiction charge presses hardest against the strict identity formulation. An idealist who says instead that blue is *dependent on* consciousness without being *identical with* it has not been shown to contradict himself — he has been shown to need a different argument, since *esse est percipi* was the argument he had.

#### 3. What "transparency" is and is not ✅

- ✅ **It is a diagnosis** of why the act/object distinction is persistently overlooked: attention passes through the act to the object, so that when we try to attend to consciousness itself we find only what we are conscious *of*.
- ⚠️ **It is not a premiss** of the refutation. The refutation runs on the comparison argument; transparency explains the psychology of the mistake.
- ❓ **It is not uncontested.** Later philosophers of mind have used the same transparency observation to argue the opposite conclusion — that there is nothing to introspect *beyond* the object, and hence no separately identifiable act. Moore's reply must be that elusiveness is not non-existence, and the structural argument of §1 is what carries that reply.

#### 4. The exact scope of the result — and the Berkeley/Hegel discrimination

| Question | Answer |
|---|---|
| Does the paper refute the *esse est percipi* argument? | ✅ Yes — the identity it needs is denied on structural grounds |
| Does it prove objects exist unperceived? | ⚠️ No. Distinguishing the object from the act shows only that the object's being is not *constituted* by the act |
| Does it refute Berkeley's whole philosophy? | ⚠️ No. It refutes the equation on which the central argument rests; Berkeley's positive system, including God's perception, is Topic 03 |
| Does it touch Hegel's Absolute Idealism? | ✅ No, and say so. Absolute Idealism does not argue from *esse est percipi*; it argues from internal relations and the incompleteness of finite determinations |
| Does it supply a theory of perception? | ⚠️ No. Moore leaves the relation between sense-data and material objects unsettled — which is exactly the unfinished analysis Session 2 said may be left open |

- ⚠️ **The comparative half of 2018 Q3(a).** Moore's reaction to Berkeley is *epistemic and distinction-drawing*: he denies the identification and protects ordinary certainty. Russell's reaction is *logical and reconstructive*: he accepts that we are directly given sense-data and then rebuilds physical objects as **logical constructions** out of them, and reduces apparent names by contextual analysis. Both reject Berkeley's conclusion; only Russell offers a replacement architecture. That single sentence is the comparison the question asks for.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** Moore's refutation of idealism is a bounded but decisive result: it destroys the argument from *esse est percipi* without establishing the mind-independence of anything.
> **Named evidence.** "The Refutation of Idealism" (*Mind*, 1903) distinguishes the act of awareness from its object and argues that "Blue is one object of sensation and green is another, and consciousness, which both sensations have in common, is different from either"; the transparency or diaphanous character of consciousness is offered as the explanation of why the two are so persistently conflated.
> **Analysis.** The argument is structural rather than introspective — on the identity thesis the two sensations would share nothing and differ in nothing — and the sharper 1903 formulation quoted in 2020 shows the identification to be self-undermining, since it must deploy the distinction between blue and consciousness in order to deny that the distinction makes a difference.
> **Qualification.** What follows is only that the object is not constituted by the act: unperceived existence requires a further argument, no theory of perception is supplied, and Absolute Idealism, which never rested on the perceptual equation, is untouched — so the honest verdict is that Moore removed idealism's most economical argument rather than idealism itself.

#### Exam link

- **2025 Q3(c)** prints the blue/green/consciousness sentence and asks for an account of the refutation "with reference to this statement": name the paper and year, give the target, run the comparison argument, add transparency, then close on Step 6.
- **2020 Q2(b)** prints the "self-contradictory mistake" sentence: §2 is the specific decoding it demands, and the residual in §2 is the "critically" half.
- **2018 Q3(a)** (Empiricism-owned) asks for the Moore/Russell difference: §4's last bullet is the comparison.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| "Moore refutes idealism" without qualification | He refutes the *esse est percipi* argument. Absolute Idealism is untouched, and mind-independence is not proved |
| Confusing 1903 *Mind* with *Principia Ethica* | Same year, different book, different subject. The naturalistic fallacy is not in this clause |
| Making transparency a premiss | It diagnoses the error; the comparison argument does the refuting |
| Paraphrasing the printed quotation | Both 2020 and 2025 print exact sentences. Reproduce them and then explain the words *self-contradictory*, *common* and *different* |
| Treating the act/object distinction as proof of realism | It blocks an identification. Realism needs Moore's *other* argument, the proof of 1939 |

#### Revision notes

- "The Refutation of Idealism", *Mind*, 1903. Target: *esse est percipi*.
- Act of awareness (common element) vs object of awareness (variable element).
- Comparison argument: on the identity thesis the two sensations share nothing and differ in nothing — both false.
- 2020 sentence: the identification is a "self-contradictory mistake" because it must use the distinction to state itself.
- Transparency/diaphanousness: a diagnosis of the error, not a premiss; ❓ later used against Moore by defenders of a no-act view.
- Bounded result: *esse est percipi* blocked ✅ · unperceived existence not proved ⚠️ · no theory of perception ⚠️ · Absolute Idealism untouched ✅.
- Moore vs Russell on Berkeley: distinction-drawing and epistemic defence against logical reconstruction by constructions and contextual analysis.
