### SESSION 8 — RUSSELL'S LOGICAL ATOMISM: ATOMIC FACTS, TRUTH-FUNCTIONS, ISOMORPHISM AND NEGATIVE FACTS

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Russell thinks the world is made of *facts*, not of things — and that complicated facts are built out of simple ones. A simple, or atomic, fact is one particular thing having a quality, or a few particular things standing in a relation. Language mirrors this: an atomic sentence states one atomic fact, and every more complicated sentence is built out of atomic ones with "and", "or", "not" and "if". The job of philosophy is to take complicated sentences apart until you reach the simple ones, and thereby to see what the world must contain.

**Technical definition:** **Logical atomism** ("The Philosophy of Logical Atomism", lectures **1918**, published in *The Monist* 1918–19) is the doctrine that (i) the world consists of **facts**; (ii) an **atomic fact** contains no other facts as constituents — a particular exemplifies a quality, or several particulars stand in a relation — and may therefore contain particulars and universals without being a microscopic or constituent-less object; (iii) an **atomic proposition** states such a fact, and **molecular propositions** are built from atomic ones by truth-functional operations, so that their truth-values are wholly determined by the truth-values of the atoms and the connectives; and (iv) a true proposition and the fact it states share the same structure — the **isomorphism thesis**. ⚠️ Russell reluctantly admits **negative facts** as truth-makers for true negative propositions, and treats the admission as a cost.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Russell's own gloss on his title is the whole doctrine and the whole defence at once: "the atoms that I wish to arrive at as the sort of last residue in analysis are **logical** atoms and not physical atoms" — atomism here is a claim about where analysis stops, not a claim about how small things are.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **fact** — what makes a proposition true or false; ⚠️ facts are not themselves true or false
- **atomic fact** — contains no other facts as constituents; particular + quality, or particulars + relation
- **atomic and molecular propositions** — the linguistic counterparts
- **truth-functionality** — no fact corresponds to "and" or "or"
- **isomorphism** — same logical structure in the true proposition and the fact
- **logical, not physical, atoms** ⚠️ — the 2020 quotation, and the trap it is set to catch
- **negative facts** ⚠️ — admitted as a truth-making cost, and contested

**How to use them:** in the 2020 question the quotation *is* the thesis; start by unpacking "last residue in analysis" and "logical, not physical", then give the structure of an atomic fact, then the molecular layer, then the negative-fact cost.

#### Plain-language visual — the two-storey building

```text
   UPPER STOREY: MOLECULAR PROPOSITIONS
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   |  "This is red AND that is blue"                                |
   |  "This is red OR that is blue"                                 |
   |  "NOT (this is red)"                                           |
   |  "IF this is red THEN that is blue"                            |
   |                                                                |
   |  TRUTH-VALUE fixed entirely by the atoms below + the connective|
   |  NO EXTRA FACT in the world corresponds to "and" or "or"       |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
                              ^
                              | built by truth-functional operations
                              |
   GROUND FLOOR: ATOMIC PROPOSITIONS
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   |  "This is red"          a PARTICULAR has a QUALITY             |
   |  "This is to the left   several PARTICULARS stand in a         |
   |   of that"              RELATION                               |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
                              ^
                              | each TRUE one corresponds to
                              |
   THE WORLD: ATOMIC FACTS
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   |  a fact containing NO OTHER FACTS as constituents              |
   |  ⚠️ but it DOES contain constituents: particulars, qualities,  |
   |     relations. "Atomic" = no facts inside, NOT no parts inside  |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
```

**In simple words:** think of a building with two floors. On the ground floor are the simplest statements — one thing being some way, or a few things standing in some relation to each other. Everything on the upper floor is made by joining ground-floor statements with logical words. Because of that, once you know which ground-floor statements are true, you automatically know which upper-floor ones are. And crucially, the logical words themselves do not name anything: there is no object in the world called "and".

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| The world is **facts**, not things | a list of objects is not a description of the world |
| Facts are **truth-makers**, not truth-bearers | ⚠️ a fact is not true; a proposition is |
| "Atomic" means **no facts inside** | not "no parts", and certainly not "microscopically small" |
| Molecular truth is **determined** | truth-functionality; connectives name nothing |
| Negative facts are a **cost** | admitted reluctantly, and contested |

> **One-line memory:** logical atoms are where analysis stops, not where the microscope stops.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — the doctrine, the 2020 sentence and the negative-fact problem

```text
   THE 2020 QUOTATION, DECODED CLAUSE BY CLAUSE
   "The reason that I call my doctrine logical atomism is because the
    atoms that I wish to arrive at as the sort of last residue in
    analysis are logical atoms and not physical atoms."
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | "last residue in analysis"  -> atomism is defined by a METHOD:  |
   |                                what survives when analysis has  |
   |                                removed every complexity         |
   | "logical ... not physical"   -> the atoms are constituents of   |
   |                                FACTS - particulars, qualities,  |
   |                                relations - not tiny bodies      |
   | "the doctrine"               -> hence an ONTOLOGY read off a    |
   |                                LOGIC, not a physics             |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
        |
   THE STRUCTURE OF AN ATOMIC FACT
   +----------------------+---------------------------------------+
   | MONADIC              | a PARTICULAR exemplifies a QUALITY     |
   |                      | "this is red"                          |
   +----------------------+---------------------------------------+
   | RELATIONAL           | PARTICULARS stand in a RELATION        |
   |                      | "this is to the left of that"          |
   +----------------------+---------------------------------------+
   ⚠️ "Atomic" = CONTAINS NO OTHER FACTS. It plainly contains
      constituents. The commonest error is to read "atomic" as
      "simple in every sense".
        |
   THE ISOMORPHISM THESIS
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   |  PROPOSITION   name1  ---- relation-word ----  name2           |
   |                  |             |                 |             |
   |                 v           v               v            |
   |  FACT         object1 ------ relation ------- object2          |
   |  SAME LOGICAL STRUCTURE. Names correspond to objects; the      |
   |  ARRANGEMENT of names corresponds to the ARRANGEMENT of        |
   |  objects.                                                      |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   ⚠️ This is the BRIDGE between language and world in Russell's
      atomism - and it is the point of nearest approach to the
      Tractatus, which is why the two atomisms are so often merged.
        |
   THE NEGATIVE-FACT PROBLEM
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | "Socrates is not alive" is TRUE. What makes it true?           |
   |   OPTION 1  a NEGATIVE FACT - that a positive state does not   |
   |             obtain. RUSSELL RELUCTANTLY ACCEPTS THIS.          |
   |   COST      absences are added to the inventory of the world;  |
   |             many philosophers find this intolerable            |
   |   WHY HE PAYS IT  to preserve a DIRECT truth-making relation:  |
   |             every truth is made true by something              |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | WITTGENSTEIN'S DIFFERENT ROUTE                                 |
   |   obtaining elementary states constitute the world, and their  |
   |   TOTALITY also determines which possible states do NOT obtain |
   |   (2.05). Negation is not a further object and the logical     |
   |   constants are not representatives (4.0312).                  |
   |   -> NO negative facts are required.                           |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
```

⚠️ **Caption.** The bottom two boxes are the sharpest available contrast between the two atomisms, and they are worth writing out whenever a question invites comparison: the same problem, two different solutions, and one of them costs an ontological category.

#### 1. Facts, and why the world is not a list of things ✅

- ✅ A **fact** is what makes a proposition true or false. Russell is explicit that facts are **not themselves true or false**: truth and falsity belong to propositions, and facts are what they answer to.
- ✅ Hence the world cannot be described by a **list of objects**. Give me a complete catalogue of every object there is and I still do not know how things stand — which of them have which qualities, and how they are related. Only facts settle that.
- ⚠️ **The point of contact with the *Tractatus*.** "The world is the totality of facts, not of things" (1.1) makes the same move. ❌ But do not therefore write that Russell and Wittgenstein held one doctrine: they agree that the world is factual and disagree about what the constituents of facts are and how we know them.

#### 2. Atomic and molecular, and what truth-functionality buys ✅

| Level | Character | Example | What settles its truth |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Atomic** ✅ | asserts one fact about one or more simples; no logical connectives | "This is red" | whether the corresponding atomic fact obtains |
| **Molecular** ✅ | built from atomic propositions with *and*, *or*, *not*, *if–then* | "This is red and that is blue" | wholly determined by the truth-values of the atoms plus the connective |

- ✅ **The pay-off.** If truth-functionality holds, the connectives need no ontological counterparts: there is no conjunctive fact in addition to the two conjuncts' facts. ⚠️ This is a large economy, and it is why "no extra facts correspond to *and* or *or*" is worth a sentence in the answer.
- ⚠️ **The exposed assumption.** The programme requires that **every** proposition be a truth-function of atomic ones. Belief-contexts ("George IV believes that…") and modal contexts resist this, and Russell knows it. Concede the difficulty rather than pretending the reduction is complete.

#### 3. Atomism and the theory of descriptions — how far the support actually goes ✅

This is the exact question of **2018 Q4(c)** and **2024 Q3(a)**, and the discrimination is where the marks are.

- ✅ **What descriptions do supply.** Contextual analysis removes **merely apparent constituents** — "the F" is shown not to be a constituent at all — and leaves quantified structure plus genuine logical constituents. That is precisely the kind of result the atomist wants: an analysis that separates real residues from grammatical illusions.
- ✅ **The methodological continuity.** Both doctrines rest on "grammatical form is not logical form"; descriptions are the demonstration that the principle has teeth, and atomism is the ontology one reaches by applying it systematically.
- ⚠️ **What descriptions do not supply.** They do **not** prove that analysis **terminates**; they do not **identify** the simples; and they do not show that reality is **exhaustively** atomic. Removing one class of pseudo-constituents is not the same as reaching a base.
- ⚠️ **The verdict sentence.** "The theory of descriptions makes logical atomism *reasonable* and does not make it *proved*: it shows how a residue is reached in a particular case, and leaves open whether every analysis reaches one."

#### 4. Criticisms, replies and the honest position ❓

| Objection | Russell's position | Residual |
|---|---|---|
| What *are* the simples? Sense-data? Only "this" and "that" qualify as logically proper names, which is extremely restrictive ✅ | He accepts the restriction openly; the base is what analysis would reach, not something he claims to have catalogued | ⚠️ The inventory was never fixed, and Russell's own candidates changed |
| Negative facts inflate the ontology with absences ⚠️ | Accepted as a cost of preserving a direct truth-making relation | ❓ Contested; Wittgenstein's totality account avoids the cost |
| Atomism is a *metaphysics read off a notation* — it assumes the world matches a particular logical symbolism ❓ | Russell's reply is that the notation is not arbitrary but the form any adequate analysis must take | ❓ This is the deepest objection and the one that later drives Quine's and Strawson's criticisms |
| Not every proposition is a truth-function of atomic ones (belief, modality) ⚠️ | Acknowledged as an unresolved difficulty | ⚠️ The same pressure recurs against the *Tractatus* at 5 and 5.54 |

- ⚠️ **The exam-safe closing position.** Russell's atomism should be assessed as a **programme** rather than as a completed system: the method of analysis is a permanent contribution, the ontological claim that analysis terminates in a fixed base was never established, and the negative-fact concession shows Russell himself paying visible costs rather than concealing them.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** Logical atomism is an ontology defined by a method: its atoms are whatever survives complete analysis, which is why Russell insists they are logical and not physical.
> **Named evidence.** In the 1918 lectures Russell writes that "the atoms that I wish to arrive at as the sort of last residue in analysis are logical atoms and not physical atoms"; an atomic fact contains no other facts as constituents — a particular exemplifying a quality, or particulars in a relation — while molecular propositions are truth-functions of atomic ones, so that no fact corresponds to "and" or "or"; and a true proposition shares the logical structure of the fact it states.
> **Analysis.** The theory of descriptions supports this programme by removing apparent constituents and leaving quantified structure plus genuine residues, and the truth-functional layer supplies a large economy by denying ontological counterparts to the connectives; together they make the atomist expectation reasonable.
> **Qualification.** The support falls short of proof — descriptions do not show that analysis terminates, identify the simples, or establish that reality is exhaustively atomic — and Russell's reluctant admission of negative facts as truth-makers for true negations, together with the resistance of belief and modal contexts to truth-functional treatment, shows the programme carrying acknowledged costs rather than closing.

#### Exam link

- **2020 Q2(c)** prints the "logical atoms and not physical atoms" sentence and asks for the nature of atomic facts in its light: §0's decoding table and §1–§2 are the answer; the negative-fact box supplies the critical close.
- **2022 Q1(b)** asks how the method of logical analysis "ultimately ends in establishing atomic theory of meaning": Sessions 5, 7 and this one in that order, with §3's qualification as the verdict.
- **2018 Q4(c)** and **2024 Q3(a)** are both answered by §3 — and both are lost by a script that claims descriptions *prove* atomism.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| "Atomic facts are very small physical facts" | ⚠️ The 2020 quotation exists to block exactly this. Atoms are logical residues |
| "Atomic means without constituents" | It means containing no other **facts**. Particulars, qualities and relations are constituents |
| "Facts are true" | Facts are truth-**makers**; propositions are truth-**bearers** |
| "Descriptions prove atomism" | They support it by removing pseudo-constituents; they do not show that analysis terminates |
| Merging Russell's atoms with Tractarian objects | Russell's are epistemically identified in this phase; Wittgenstein's are postulated by conditions of sense and never exemplified |
| Omitting negative facts | It is the point where Russell visibly pays a price, and the easiest assessment mark in the session |

#### Revision notes

- 1918 lectures, published *The Monist* 1918–19; Russell's preface credits the ideas to Wittgenstein.
- World = facts. Facts are truth-makers, not truth-bearers. A list of objects does not describe the world.
- Atomic fact: no other facts as constituents; particular + quality, or particulars + relation.
- Atomic / molecular propositions; truth-functionality; no fact answers to "and" or "or".
- Isomorphism: true proposition and fact share logical structure; names ↔ objects, arrangement ↔ arrangement.
- 2020 sentence: "last residue in analysis"; logical, not physical.
- Negative facts: accepted reluctantly to preserve direct truth-making; contested. Wittgenstein's totality account (2.05, 4.0312) avoids them.
- Descriptions support but do not prove: no proof of termination, no identification of simples, no exhaustive atomism.

#### CLOSING RECALL FLOW — LOGICAL ATOMISM

```text
1918 "THE PHILOSOPHY OF LOGICAL ATOMISM" (preface credits Wittgenstein)
        |
        v
WORLD = FACTS, not things. FACTS are TRUTH-MAKERS, not truth-bearers
        |
        v
ATOMIC FACT = contains NO OTHER FACTS
   monadic: particular + quality | relational: particulars + relation
   ⚠️ it DOES have constituents; "atomic" is not "partless"
        |
        v
ATOMIC PROPOSITION <-> atomic fact
MOLECULAR PROPOSITION = TRUTH-FUNCTION of atomic ones
   -> NO fact corresponds to "and" or "or"
        |
        v
ISOMORPHISM: same logical structure in true proposition and fact
        |
        v
2020 SENTENCE: "last residue in ANALYSIS ... LOGICAL atoms and NOT
   PHYSICAL atoms"
        |
        v
DESCRIPTIONS -> remove pseudo-constituents -> SUPPORT the atomist search
   BUT do NOT prove termination | identify simples | establish exhaustive
   atomism
        |
        v
COST: NEGATIVE FACTS accepted reluctantly to keep direct truth-making
   WITTGENSTEIN'S ROUTE: totality of obtaining states settles the rest;
   logical constants are not representatives (4.0312)
        |
        v
VERDICT: assess as a PROGRAMME - permanent method, unfixed inventory,
   visible costs
ANSWER LINE: logical atoms are where analysis stops, not where the
   microscope stops
```

### SESSION 9 — THE *TRACTATUS*: FACTS, OBJECTS, STATES OF AFFAIRS AND THE PICTURE THEORY OF MEANING

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Wittgenstein starts not from things but from *how things stand*. The world, he says, is the totality of facts, not of things. A proposition is meaningful because it works like a picture: its parts stand for objects, and the way its parts are arranged says that the objects are arranged that way too. If they are, the proposition is true; if they are not, it is false. Either way it has *sense*, because it presents a possible situation.

**Technical definition:** In the *Tractatus Logico-Philosophicus* (German text **1921**; bilingual edition **1922**), ✅ *"The world is the totality of facts, not of things"* (**1.1**). A **state of affairs (*Sachverhalt*)** is a possible combination of **objects**; the obtaining of states of affairs constitutes the facts that make up the world. A proposition is a **logical picture (*Bild*)** of a possible state of affairs: its elements correspond to objects, and its structure **depicts (*abbildet*)** a possible arrangement of them. **Pictorial form (*Form der Abbildung*)** is what a picture must share with reality in order to depict it in its particular medium; **logical form (*logische Form*)** is the form every picture of any medium must share with reality, and is therefore the form of all possible representation. ⚠️ Logical form is not itself a depictable fact: it **shows** itself in every meaningful proposition and cannot be **said**.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The picture theory is not the observation that language resembles what it describes but the far stronger claim that resemblance of *content* is irrelevant and only shared *logical form* matters — which is why the theory ends by discovering something no proposition can ever state.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **the world is the totality of facts, not of things** — 1.1, safe verbatim in both translations
- **state of affairs (*Sachverhalt*)** ⚠️ — flag the Ogden "atomic fact" against Pears–McGuinness "state of affairs"
- **object** — a logical simple, characterised by its **possibilities of combination**, never exemplified
- **logical picture (*Bild*)**, **to depict (*abbilden*)** — the *Bild* vocabulary; "picture theory" is the commentators' label
- **pictorial form (*Form der Abbildung*)** against **logical form (*logische Form*)** — the 2022 question in five words
- **projective relation** — the method of projection that makes a propositional sign into a proposition
- **shown, not said** — the closing move of the session, developed fully in Session 11

**How to use them:** 2022 Q3(c) asks a two-part question — is there a difference between pictorial and logical form, and how does logical form define the relation between language and reality? Answer the first with a contrast, the second with the *universality* of logical form, and close on showing.

#### Plain-language visual — the model in the courtroom

```text
   THE ORIGIN STORY WITTGENSTEIN'S PICTURE THEORY IS OFTEN TOLD WITH
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | In a Paris courtroom a road accident is reconstructed with     |
   | model cars and dolls. Nobody thinks the model IS the accident. |
   | Yet everyone understands what it says.                         |
   |                                                                |
   | WHY? Because the model's PARTS stand for the real parts, and   |
   | the ARRANGEMENT of the model's parts says that the real parts  |
   | were arranged like THAT.                                       |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
                              |
                              v
   THE GENERALISATION
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | The model works even though it is made of wood and the         |
   | accident was made of steel. What it shares with the accident   |
   | is not MATERIAL but STRUCTURE.                                 |
   |                                                                |
   | A SPATIAL model shares SPATIAL form.                           |
   | A MUSICAL score shares ACOUSTIC form with the music.           |
   | A PROPOSITION shares LOGICAL form with the fact.               |
   |                                                                |
   | And LOGICAL form is the one form EVERY picture must share,     |
   | whatever its medium - because it is the form of representation |
   | as such.                                                       |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
                              |
                              v
   THE CONSEQUENCE THAT ENDS THE BOOK
   To depict logical form you would need a picture standing OUTSIDE
   logical form - i.e. outside the possibility of representation.
   There is no such position. So logical form can only SHOW itself.
```

**In simple words:** a picture works by *sharing something* with what it pictures. A map of Delhi shares spatial arrangement with Delhi; a musical score shares a structure with the sound. Every picture, whatever it is made of, must share at least *logical* structure with what it shows — otherwise it could not represent it at all. And that is why we can never draw a picture of logical structure: any picture we drew would already have to use it.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Facts, not things | 1.1; a catalogue of objects is not a description of the world |
| Objects are **simples** | characterised by combinatorial possibility; ⚠️ never exemplified in the text |
| A proposition **depicts** | its structure presents a possible arrangement of objects |
| Pictorial form is **medium-specific** | logical form is **universal** |
| Truth is **comparison** | "to tell whether a picture is true or false we must compare it with reality" (2.223) |

> **One-line memory:** the picture shares structure, not stuff — and the deepest structure it shares can never itself be pictured.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — the ontology, the picturing relation, and the two forms

```text
   THE ONTOLOGY OF THE OPENING PROPOSITIONS
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | 1     The world is all that is the case.                       |
   | 1.1   The world is the totality of FACTS, not of THINGS.       |
   | 1.13  The facts in LOGICAL SPACE are the world.                |
   | 2     What is the case, a fact, is the existence of STATES OF  |
   |       AFFAIRS.                                                 |
   | 2.01  A state of affairs is a combination of OBJECTS.          |
   | 2.013 Each thing is, as it were, in a SPACE of possible states |
   |       of affairs. Possibility is built INTO the object.        |
   | 2.06  The existence and non-existence of states of affairs is  |
   |       reality.                                                 |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
        |
   TRANSLATION CONTROL ⚠️
   Sachverhalt = "atomic fact" (Ogden/Ramsey) = "state of affairs"
   (Pears-McGuinness). CHOOSE ONE AND STAY WITH IT. And do NOT equate
   Tractarian objects with Russell's epistemically identified atoms.
        |
   THE PICTURING RELATION - three requirements
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | (i)   ELEMENTS of the picture (names) correspond to OBJECTS.   |
   | (ii)  The ARRANGEMENT of the elements depicts an arrangement   |
   |       of the objects.                                          |
   | (iii) A METHOD OF PROJECTION connects sign to situation - the  |
   |       propositional SIGN plus its projective relation to the   |
   |       world is the PROPOSITION (3.11-3.12).                    |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | TRUE  if the depicted state of affairs obtains                 |
   | FALSE if it does not                                           |
   | EITHER WAY MEANINGFUL, because it depicts a POSSIBLE           |
   | arrangement (2.201-2.203, 2.221-2.224)                         |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
        |
   THE TWO FORMS - the 2022 question, in a table
   +---------------------------+-----------------------------------+
   | PICTORIAL FORM            | LOGICAL FORM                      |
   | (Form der Abbildung)      | (logische Form)                   |
   +---------------------------+-----------------------------------+
   | what a picture must share | the MOST ABSTRACT structural       |
   | with reality to depict it | commonality - what EVERY picture,  |
   | AT ALL, in its own medium | in ANY medium, must share with     |
   |                           | reality                            |
   +---------------------------+-----------------------------------+
   | a spatial model shares    | every meaningful proposition       |
   | SPATIAL form; a score     | shares LOGICAL form with the fact  |
   | shares ACOUSTIC form      | it depicts                         |
   +---------------------------+-----------------------------------+
   | a picture can depict only | a proposition can depict ANY fact  |
   | what shares ITS form      | whatever, because logical form is  |
   |                           | the UNIVERSAL medium               |
   +---------------------------+-----------------------------------+
   RELATION: logical form is the form pictorial forms have in common.
   Every picture is a LOGICAL picture (2.182); not every picture is a
   spatial or a musical one.
        |
   AND THE CONSEQUENCE
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | Logical form is NOT itself a fact that can be depicted. Any    |
   | depiction of it would already presuppose it. It therefore      |
   | SHOWS itself in every meaningful proposition and cannot be     |
   | SAID (4.12, 4.121, 4.1212).  -> SESSION 11                     |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
```

⚠️ **Caption.** The 2022 paper asks precisely for the middle table plus the bottom box. A script that gives only "a proposition pictures a fact" has answered neither half of the question.

#### 1. Why the world is facts and not things ✅

- ✅ **The argument.** A complete inventory of objects leaves everything undecided: it does not tell you which qualities they have or how they are related. What is the case is a matter of **configuration**, and a configuration is a fact. Hence 1.1.
- ✅ **Objects and their possibilities.** An object is characterised not by an intrinsic description but by the **states of affairs into which it can enter** (2.0121, 2.013). Possibility is written into the object; the space of possible configurations is fixed once the objects are fixed.
- ⚠️ **The honest gap.** Wittgenstein gives **no uncontested example** of a simple object or an elementary proposition. The text argues that simples are *required* — determinate sense demands a final base — rather than exhibiting them. ⚠️ Say this explicitly; it is a standing criticism, and pre-empting it earns the assessment mark.
- ⚠️ **The contrast with Russell.** Russell's atoms are identified epistemically, through acquaintance, in the 1918 phase. Wittgenstein's objects are postulated by a **condition-of-sense argument** and are never identified. Two atomisms, one word.

#### 2. What "picture" does and does not mean ✅

- ✅ **It is a technical notion.** A picture is anything that shares form with what it represents and whose elements are correlated with elements of the represented situation. Resemblance in the everyday sense is **irrelevant**; a musical score does not look like music.
- ✅ **The propositional sign and the method of projection.** A written or spoken sentence is a **propositional sign** — a fact in its own right, since its elements stand in a determinate relation. The sign together with its **projective relation** to the world is the **proposition** (3.11–3.12). Meaning is not a mental accompaniment; it is a projection.
- ⚠️ **Provenance discipline.** The label "picture theory" is the **commentators'**. Wittgenstein writes of *Bild* — picture or model — and of *abbilden*, to depict. Write "the doctrine usually called the picture theory" once, and the provenance mark is secured.
- ⚠️ **What the theory explains that a name-theory cannot.** How a sentence can be understood before you know whether it is true. On a naming model, an understood sentence would need an existing object to correspond to it; on the picturing model it needs only a **possible** configuration. That is why falsity is not a failure of meaning.

#### 3. Pictorial form against logical form — the discrimination the 2022 paper wants ✅

| Question | Pictorial form | Logical form |
|---|---|---|
| What is it? | what a picture must have in common with reality **to depict it at all in its medium** | the most abstract structural commonality — what **every** picture shares with reality |
| Example | a spatial model shares spatial form; a score shares acoustic form | every proposition shares logical form with the fact it depicts |
| Reach | limited to what shares that medium's form | unlimited: a proposition can depict **any** fact whatever |
| Status | a feature of a kind of picture | the form of **all possible representation** |
| Can it be depicted? | ✅ a spatial form can be represented in another medium | ⚠️ no — any depiction would presuppose it |

- ✅ **The second half of the question — how logical form defines the relation between language and reality.** Because logical form is common to proposition and fact, language and world are not two realms needing a bridge: they already share the structure that makes depiction possible. ✅ **2.18:** what a picture must have in common with reality in order to depict it at all is logical form. The relation is therefore **internal** — constitutive of the proposition — not an external correlation set up by convention or by causal contact.
- ⚠️ **And the price.** An internal relation cannot be described from outside. That is the transition to Session 11: the very thing that explains meaning turns out to be unsayable.

#### 4. What the picture theory rules out — stated before Session 10 develops it ✅

1. A proposition that depicts **no possible situation** is not false; it is **nonsensical (*unsinnig*)**.
2. A "proposition" that could not be false depicts no situation either; tautologies and contradictions are **senseless (*sinnlos*)** — well-formed but empty. (Session 10.)
3. Statements about the **relation between language and reality** attempt to depict the depicting relation itself, and fail. (Session 11.)
4. ⚠️ **None of this is the verification principle.** The *Tractatus* asks whether a proposition depicts a **possible fact**, not whether it can be **empirically checked**. The Vienna Circle's criterion is a later transformation of these resources and belongs to Topic 07.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** The picture theory grounds meaning in shared structure rather than in reference or resemblance, and in doing so it makes falsity a normal case rather than a defect.
> **Named evidence.** The *Tractatus* opens by declaring that "the world is the totality of facts, not of things" (1.1) and that a fact is the existence of states of affairs, each a combination of objects (2, 2.01); a proposition is a logical picture whose elements are correlated with objects and whose arrangement depicts a possible arrangement of them, so that "in order to tell whether a picture is true or false we must compare it with reality" (2.223) and "it is impossible to tell from the picture alone whether it is true or false" (2.224).
> **Analysis.** Because what the picture shares with reality is form and not material, the theory explains how a sentence can be fully understood before its truth-value is known: understanding requires only that a possible configuration be presented, which is why *pictorial* form varies with the medium while *logical* form, being the form of representation as such, is shared by every picture and every fact.
> **Qualification.** The account is bought at the cost of two commitments the text never discharges — it supplies no uncontested example of a simple object or an elementary proposition, and it makes logical form itself indescribable, since any picture of it would already have to employ it — so the picture theory succeeds as an account of sense precisely by generating the say/show problem it ends with.

#### Exam link

- **2022 Q3(c)** is this session end to end: the pictorial/logical contrast table, then the "how logical form defines the relation" answer in §3, then one closing sentence on showing.
- **2018 Q3(c)** and **2021 Q4(c)** both need §2 and §3 as the *derivation* of the say/show distinction; without it, Session 11 reads as assertion.
- **2025 Q3(a)** (Later-Wittgenstein-owned) asks about the transition from the early to the later view; this session supplies the "from" — and the boundary note in §4.4 keeps the answer honest.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| "Language is a picture of the world" without qualification | ⚠️ It is a **logical** picture; shared **form**, not resemblance. The unqualified sentence is a marked error |
| Treating *Sachverhalt* inconsistently | Ogden: "atomic fact"; Pears–McGuinness: "state of affairs". Choose one and say which |
| Identifying Tractarian objects with Russell's sense-data | Wittgenstein's objects are postulated by conditions of sense and never exemplified |
| Conflating pictorial and logical form | Pictorial form is medium-specific; logical form is the form of all representation |
| Reading the theory as verificationism | It asks about depicting a **possible** situation, not about empirical checkability. Topic 07 owns the criterion |
| Dating the English text 1921 | German 1921; bilingual English–German 1922, with the Latin title suggested by Moore |

#### Revision notes

- 1.1 "The world is the totality of facts, not of things"; 1.13 facts in logical space; 2 facts are the existence of states of affairs; 2.01 a state of affairs is a combination of objects; 2.013 possibility is built into the object.
- *Sachverhalt* ⚠️ Ogden "atomic fact" / Pears–McGuinness "state of affairs".
- Proposition = propositional sign + projective relation (3.11–3.12); elements ↔ objects; arrangement depicts arrangement.
- True if the state of affairs obtains, false if not, meaningful either way (2.201–2.224).
- Pictorial form: medium-specific. Logical form: universal, the form of all representation (2.18); every picture is a logical picture (2.182).
- Logical form is shown, not said → Session 11.
- ⚠️ No uncontested example of a simple object; the base is argued for, not exhibited.
- ⚠️ "Picture theory" is the commentators' label; Wittgenstein writes *Bild* and *abbilden*.

#### CLOSING RECALL FLOW — THE PICTURE THEORY

```text
1.1 THE WORLD IS THE TOTALITY OF FACTS, NOT OF THINGS
        |
        v
FACT = existence of STATES OF AFFAIRS (Sachverhalte) = combinations of
   OBJECTS. Possibility is built INTO the object (2.013)
   ⚠️ objects are never exemplified - argued for, not exhibited
        |
        v
PROPOSITION = propositional SIGN + PROJECTIVE RELATION (3.11-3.12)
   elements <-> objects | arrangement depicts arrangement
        |
        v
TRUE if the state of affairs obtains | FALSE if not | MEANINGFUL either
   way, because a POSSIBLE arrangement is presented (2.221-2.224)
        |

        vs

   every picture is a LOGICAL picture (2.182)
        |

HOW LOGICAL FORM DEFINES THE LANGUAGE-WORLD RELATION: it is COMMON to
   both, so the relation is INTERNAL, not an external convention

   presupposes it -> IT SHOWS ITSELF (4.12-4.1212) -> SESSION 11
        |

ANSWER LINE: the picture shares structure, not stuff
```

### SESSION 10 — BIPOLARITY, LOGICAL SPACE, THE GENERAL FORM OF THE PROPOSITION, AND THE TWO WAYS OF FAILING TO SAY ANYTHING

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** For a sentence to say something, it has to be able to be true *and* able to be false. If it could not possibly be false, it is not telling you how things are — it is just spinning. Wittgenstein turns this into a whole system: every proposition marks out a place in the total space of possibilities, and every proposition whatever can be generated from a base of simplest propositions by one repeated logical operation. Sentences that fail this test fail in two different ways, and the difference between the two failures is examinable.

**Technical definition:** **Bipolarity** is the condition of sense: a proposition has **sense (*Sinn*)** only if it is capable of being true **and** capable of being false. **Logical space (*logischer Raum*)** is the totality of possible configurations; a proposition "determines a place in logical space" (**3.4**), so that understanding *p* is understanding what it excludes. The **general form of the proposition** (**6**) is ✅ **[ p̄ , ξ̄ , N(ξ̄) ]** — the rule generating every proposition from the totality of elementary propositions by repeated application of the **N-operator**, joint negation. Two failure modes follow: a **tautology** or **contradiction** is **senseless (*sinnlos*)** — well-formed, but saying nothing about the world; a metaphysical pseudo-proposition is **nonsensical (*unsinnig*)** — it depicts no possible situation at all.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Wittgenstein's account of sense is a single condition worked out with unusual ruthlessness: a proposition says something only if the world could refute it — and once that is granted, logic's own truths turn out to say nothing, and metaphysics turns out not to be saying anything at all.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **bipolarity** — capable of truth **and** capable of falsity; ⚠️ stronger than bivalence
- **sense (*Sinn*)** — what a proposition has when it presents a possible situation
- **logical space (*logischer Raum*)** — the totality of possibilities; a proposition determines a **place** in it
- **the fundamental idea (*Grundgedanke*)** — 4.0312, "the logical constants are not representatives"
- **the general form of the proposition** — 6, and the informal 4.5, "this is how things stand"
- **N-operator** — joint negation: none of these is true
- ***sinnlos*** against ***unsinnig*** — the two failures; the single most tested discrimination in the *Tractatus*

**How to use them:** treat bipolarity, logical space and the general form as **one doctrine seen from three sides**, and say so. That framing alone lifts an answer, because most scripts present them as three unrelated pieces of machinery.

#### Plain-language visual — the map with every possible route

```text
   THINK OF ALL THE WAYS THE WORLD COULD BE, LAID OUT AS A GRID
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   |  . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .  |
   |  . . . . . [ REGION WHERE p IS TRUE ] . . . . . . . . . . . .  |
   |  . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .  |
   |  . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .  |
   |  . . . . . [ REGION WHERE p IS FALSE ] . . . . . . . . . . .  |
   |  . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .  |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
        A MEANINGFUL PROPOSITION DIVIDES THE GRID.
        To understand p is to know WHICH SIDE you are being put on -
        which is also to understand not-p. SAME PLACE, OPPOSITE POLE.
   =====================================================================
   NOW THE TWO FAILURES
   +-------------------------------+-------------------------------+
   | TAUTOLOGY covers the WHOLE    | CONTRADICTION covers NONE of  |
   | grid. It excludes nothing.    | the grid. It excludes         |
   | "It is raining or it is not." | everything.                   |
   | Nothing is ruled out, so      | "It is raining and it is not."|
   | nothing is SAID.              | Nothing is left, so nothing   |
   |          = SINNLOS            | is SAID.    = SINNLOS         |
   +-------------------------------+-------------------------------+
   | NONSENSE is not on the grid at all. "The Absolute is perfect"  |
   | does not divide the possibilities, because it never entered    |
   | the space of possibilities.                 = UNSINNIG         |
   +-------------------------------+-------------------------------+
```

**In simple words:** a claim earns its keep by ruling something out. "It will rain tomorrow" rules out a dry tomorrow — that is what makes it informative. "It will either rain or not rain" rules out nothing at all, so although it is perfectly well-formed and certainly true, it tells you nothing. And a sentence like "the Absolute is perfect" does not even manage to rule nothing out; it never got into the business of ruling things in or out, because it does not describe any possible arrangement of anything.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Bipolarity is not bivalence | bivalence: it has one of two values. Bipolarity: it must be able to have **either** |
| A proposition **excludes** | to understand *p* is to understand ¬*p* |
| Logic's truths say nothing | tautologies are *sinnlos*, not *unsinnig* |
| Metaphysics is a **different** failure | *unsinnig*: no possible situation depicted |
| One operation generates everything | N, joint negation, applied repeatedly |

> **One-line memory:** if the world could not refute it, it did not tell you anything.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — the three sides of one doctrine

```text
   SIDE 1  BIPOLARITY - the CONDITION of sense
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | A proposition has SENSE only if it CAN be true and CAN be      |
   | false.                                                         |
   | ANCHORS 4.06 "A proposition can be true or false only by being |
   |   a picture of reality"; 2.223 "to tell whether a picture is   |
   |   true or false we must compare it with reality"; 2.224 "it is |
   |   impossible to tell from the picture alone whether it is true |
   |   or false". The TERM comes from the Notes on Logic (1913).    |
   | WHY IT DOES SO MUCH WORK                                       |
   |  1 to depict is to represent something AS being the case       |
   |  2 to represent something as being the case is to allow that   |
   |    it might NOT be the case                                    |
   |  3 so sense requires BOTH truth-values to be genuinely         |
   |    available                                                   |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
        |
   SIDE 2  LOGICAL SPACE - WHERE the possibilities live
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+

   |   2.11 "a picture presents a situation in logical space";      |
   |   3.4 "a proposition determines a place in logical space";     |

   |   SYSTEM is to points: to specify a point you need the whole   |

   | CONSEQUENCES                                                   |

   |   THE WORLD IS DETERMINATE: 2.013, possibility is built into   |
   |     the object                                                 |
   |   LIMITS OF LANGUAGE = LIMITS OF THE WORLD: 5.6                |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
        |
   SIDE 3  THE GENERAL FORM OF THE PROPOSITION - the RULE
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | (a) INFORMAL, 4.5: the general form of the proposition is      |
   |     "THIS IS HOW THINGS STAND" (Es verhaelt sich so und so).   |
   |     ⚠️ Ogden renders it "Such and such is the case". FLAG THE  |
   |     RENDERING.                                                 |
   | (b) FORMAL, 6:            [ p , x , N(x) ]                     |
   |       p (p-bar)  the totality of ELEMENTARY propositions       |
   |       x (xi-bar) any arbitrarily selected SET of propositions  |
   |                  already formed                                |
   |       N(x)       the result of applying the N-OPERATOR         |
   | THE N-OPERATOR (5.502, 5.51) = JOINT NEGATION: none of the     |
   |   selected propositions is true.                               |
   |     not-p   = N(p)                                             |
   |     p or q  = N(N(p,q))                                        |
   |     p and q = N(N(p), N(q))                                    |
   |   Quantification is handled through sets of values of          |
   |   propositional functions (5.52); ⚠️ the adequacy of the       |
   |   notation for infinite or multiply general cases is DISPUTED. |
   | WHAT PROPOSITION 6 ASSERTS                                     |
   |   1 every proposition is a TRUTH-FUNCTION of elementary        |
   |     propositions (5)                                           |
   |   2 every truth-function is producible by repeated application |
   |     of ONE operation, N                                        |
   |   3 so there is ONE general form, that of a FORMAL SERIES      |
   |   4 so LOGIC NEEDS NO PRIMITIVE PROPOSITIONS AND NO LOGICAL    |
   |     OBJECTS - the decisive break with Russell and Frege        |
   |   5 so "there can be no surprises in logic" (6.1251) and       |
   |     logic's propositions are TAUTOLOGIES (6.1)                 |
   |   6 so whatever cannot be produced by this rule is NOT A       |
   |     PROPOSITION -> the general form is the CRITERION OF THE    |
   |     SAYABLE                                                    |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
```

⚠️ **Caption.** Read the three boxes as one argument: bipolarity says what sense requires, logical space says what a sense-bearing proposition *does*, and the general form says how every such proposition is built. Point 6 of the last box is the bridge to Session 11.

#### 1. The two failure modes, side by side ✅

| Term | Status | Example | Cognitive value |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Tautology** — senseless (*sinnlos*) ✅ | true under every truth-condition; says nothing about the world | "It is raining or it is not raining" | logically valid but factually empty; ❌ **not** nonsensical |
| **Contradiction** — senseless (*sinnlos*) ✅ | false under every truth-condition | "It is raining and it is not raining" | a limiting case that says nothing |
| **Metaphysical pseudo-proposition** — nonsensical (*unsinnig*) ✅ | fails the conditions of factual sense altogether | "The Absolute is perfect"; "There are objects" | not a truth-functional picture of any possible fact |

- ✅ **Why tautologies are not nonsense.** They are perfectly well-formed and belong to the symbolism; they are the **limiting cases** of proposition-formation and they **show** the scaffolding of logic (4.461–4.4611). Wittgenstein's account of logical truth is thus that logic has no subject matter of its own.
- ⚠️ **Why the distinction is examinable.** The *sinnlos/unsinnig* pair is the single most frequently mishandled item in *Tractatus* answers, and it is also the point where scripts wrongly import the verification principle: the Vienna Circle's criterion tests for empirical checkability, whereas Wittgenstein's test is whether a possible situation is depicted at all.

#### 2. Elementary propositions and their independence — the assumption under pressure ⚠️❓

✅ **The commitment.** Elementary propositions consist of names in immediate combination and are **logically independent** of one another (2.061, 4.211): no elementary proposition follows from or excludes another, so every assignment of truth-values to them represents a possible configuration. This is stronger than ordinary bipolarity and it is what makes the truth-table method exhaustive.

❓ **The colour-exclusion problem.** "This patch is red all over" and "This patch is blue all over" are incompatible, yet neither looks like a truth-function of the other, and neither looks further analysable. If both are elementary, independence fails; if neither is, the text owes an analysis it does not supply.

⚠️ **How to state it safely.** The case **pressures** the independence assumption and helped drive Wittgenstein's 1929 "Some Remarks on Logical Form" — the only philosophical article besides the *Tractatus* published in his lifetime, and one whose proposed analysis he quickly abandoned. ❌ It does **not** by itself prove that no deeper analysis could preserve independence, and ❌ it must not be used as a licence to import meaning-as-use, which belongs to Topic 08.

#### 3. The four standing presuppositions of the whole apparatus ⚠️

1. **There are elementary propositions and simple objects** — although no uncontested example is given.
2. **Elementary propositions are logically independent** — which colour exclusion seriously pressures.
3. **All propositions are truth-functions of elementary ones** — while belief-contexts and modality create difficulties. (Wittgenstein's own treatment of "A believes that p" at 5.54–5.542 is itself contested.)
4. **The N-operator can handle infinite domains** — which remains disputed.

⚠️ **Write them as pressures and later developments, not as refutations conceded inside the text.** The *Tractatus* does not admit these as failures; it is later work, including Wittgenstein's own, that treats them as such.

#### 4. Why this session is the hinge of the whole *Tractatus* ✅

- ✅ **Backwards:** bipolarity is the picture theory stated as a *condition* rather than as a *model*. A picture that could not be false would not be presenting a situation at all.
- ✅ **Forwards:** the general form is simultaneously the rule that generates every proposition and the **criterion of the sayable**. Whatever the rule cannot produce is not a proposition — and that is exactly the boundary the Preface describes when it says that the limit can be drawn only *in language*, and that what lies on the other side will simply be nonsense.
- ⚠️ **The self-application problem is therefore built in.** Propositions 5 and 6 are not themselves truth-functions of elementary propositions. Session 11 takes up what Wittgenstein does about that.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** Bipolarity, logical space and the general form of the proposition are not three doctrines but one: an account of sense as a determinate exclusion within a fixed field of possibilities.
> **Named evidence.** The *Tractatus* holds that a proposition can be true or false only by being a picture of reality (4.06) and that one cannot tell from the picture alone which it is (2.224); that a proposition determines a place in logical space (3.4) and is "like a solid body that restricts the freedom of movement of others" (4.463); that the logical constants are not representatives — Wittgenstein's own *fundamental idea* (4.0312); and that the general form of the proposition is [ p̄ , ξ̄ , N(ξ̄) ] (6), generated by repeated joint negation (5.502, 5.51).
> **Analysis.** Together these yield the two failure modes that any *Tractatus* answer must keep apart: a tautology or contradiction is *sinnlos*, well-formed but excluding nothing or everything, and therefore showing logic's scaffolding rather than describing the world, while a metaphysical sentence is *unsinnig* because it never entered logical space at all; and because logic needs no primitive propositions or logical objects, "there can be no surprises in logic" (6.1251).
> **Qualification.** The apparatus rests on four undischarged commitments — that there are simple objects and elementary propositions, that the latter are logically independent, that every proposition is truth-functional, and that the N-operator handles infinite domains — and colour exclusion pressures the second so directly that Wittgenstein revisited it in 1929, which is why the system is best presented as a rigorous programme with identifiable strain points rather than as a completed proof.

#### Exam link

- **2022 Q3(c)** is completed by this session: after the pictorial/logical form contrast, bipolarity explains *why* a proposition must be able to be false, and that is what makes the picture an account of **sense**.
- **2021 Q4(c)** and **2018 Q3(c)** need §1's *sinnlos/unsinnig* table before the say/show distinction can be stated accurately.
- **2023 Q4(c)** and **2025 Q3(a)** (both Later-Wittgenstein-owned) ask about the transition; §2 supplies the *honest* version of the pressure without importing the later doctrine.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| "*Sinnlos* = *unsinnig*" | ⚠️ The single most tested discrimination here. Tautologies are empty but well-formed; metaphysical sentences depict nothing |
| Treating bipolarity as bivalence | Bivalence: it has one of two values. Bipolarity: it must be **able** to have either |
| "Logical constants stand for logical objects" | 4.0312 denies exactly this, and calls the denial his **fundamental idea** |
| Writing the general form without explaining N | N is **joint negation**: none of the selected propositions is true. Show ¬p, v and & derived from it |
| Presenting colour exclusion as a proof of failure | It is a pressure that motivated revision; do not smuggle in meaning-as-use |
| Attributing verificationism to 6 or to 4.5 | The criterion is depicting a possible situation, not empirical checkability |

#### Revision notes

- Bipolarity: sense requires capability of truth **and** of falsity. Term from *Notes on Logic* (1913). Anchors 4.06, 2.223, 2.224.
- Logical space: 1.13, 2.11, 3.4, 3.41, 3.42; the coordinate-system analogy; 4.463 the proposition as a solid body restricting movement.
- *Grundgedanke* 4.0312: the logical constants are not representatives; negation names nothing.
- General form: informal 4.5 "this is how things stand" ⚠️ (Ogden: "such and such is the case"); formal 6 [ p̄ , ξ̄ , N(ξ̄) ]; N = joint negation (5.502, 5.51); quantification via sets of values (5.52) ⚠️ adequacy disputed.
- Consequences: every proposition a truth-function (5) · one operation · one general form · no primitive propositions or logical objects — the break with Russell and Frege · no surprises in logic (6.1251) · logic's propositions are tautologies (6.1) · the criterion of the sayable.
- *Sinnlos* (tautology, contradiction: well-formed, empty, shows logic's scaffolding, 4.461–4.4611) vs *unsinnig* (metaphysical pseudo-proposition).
- Four presuppositions: simples · independence of elementary propositions · universal truth-functionality (belief-contexts 5.54–5.542) · N over infinite domains. Colour exclusion (1929) pressures the second.

#### CLOSING RECALL FLOW — SENSE, SPACE AND THE GENERAL FORM

```text
BIPOLARITY: sense requires CAN-BE-TRUE and CAN-BE-FALSE (4.06, 2.224)
   1 depicting = representing AS being the case
   2 which allows that it might NOT be the case
   3 so BOTH truth-values must be available
        |
        v
LOGICAL SPACE: a proposition DETERMINES A PLACE (3.4); it EXCLUDES
   -> to understand p is to understand not-p
   -> 4.0312 GRUNDGEDANKE: logical constants are NOT representatives
   -> 5.6 the limits of language are the limits of the world
        |
        v
GENERAL FORM (4.5 informal | 6 formal): [ p , x , N(x) ]
   N = JOINT NEGATION: not-p = N(p) | p or q = N(N(p,q)) |
   p and q = N(N(p),N(q))
   -> one operation | no primitive propositions | no logical objects
   -> "no surprises in logic" (6.1251); logic's propositions are
      TAUTOLOGIES (6.1)
   -> THE CRITERION OF THE SAYABLE
        |
        v
TWO FAILURES
   SINNLOS   tautology / contradiction - well-formed, excludes nothing
             or everything, SHOWS logic's scaffolding (4.461-4.4611)
   UNSINNIG  metaphysical pseudo-proposition - never entered logical
             space at all
        |
        v
PRESSURES (state as pressures, not concessions): simples unexemplified |
   COLOUR EXCLUSION vs independence (1929) | belief-contexts 5.54 |
   N over infinite domains
ANSWER LINE: if the world could not refute it, it did not tell you
   anything
```

### SESSION 11 — SAYING AND SHOWING: THE LIMIT OF LANGUAGE, THE MYSTICAL, THE LADDER AND PROPOSITION 7

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Propositions tell us how things stand. But there is something a proposition cannot tell us: what makes it capable of telling us anything at all. Its logical form, the fact that it has that form, the existence of the objects it presupposes — these are not further facts to be reported; they display themselves in the proposition. Wittgenstein calls this the difference between what can be **said** and what can only be **shown**. Because ethics, aesthetics and the meaning of life are also not facts in the world, they too fall on the showing side — and so, embarrassingly, do the sentences of the *Tractatus* itself.

**Technical definition:** The **saying/showing distinction** separates what a proposition **states (*gesagt*)** — contingent states of affairs, the propositions of natural science — from what a proposition **displays (*gezeigt*)** — its own logical form, the logical properties of the world, the existence of objects presupposed by significant language, and, at the limit, value. ✅ *"What can be shown, cannot be said"* (**4.1212**). The distinction is **forced**, not stipulated: logical form is presupposed by every depiction, so any attempted depiction of it would already employ it. The *Tractatus* therefore closes with the **ladder** (**6.54**) — its own propositions are recognised as nonsensical by the reader who has used them to climb beyond them — and with **proposition 7**, which is uncommented and stands alone. ⚠️ There is **no proposition 7.1**.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Wittgenstein's silence is not modesty, mysticism or reticence but the exact consequence of his own account of sense: since every proposition works by sharing logical form with a possible situation, the sharing itself can never be one more situation to be described — and what cannot be described cannot be said badly either, which is why the correct response is silence rather than caution.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **said (*gesagt*) against shown (*gezeigt*)** — the distinction, in Wittgenstein's own vocabulary
- **4.1212** — "what can be shown, cannot be said"; safe verbatim
- **the limits of my language mean the limits of my world** — 5.6; the limit is drawn **from inside**
- **the mystical (*das Mystische*)** — 6.522: it shows itself; ⚠️ not "the emotional" or "the religious" loosely
- **the ladder** — 6.54, and the self-application problem it names
- **proposition 7** ⚠️ — quote it *and* name the translation; there is no 7.1
- **standard ineffability against resolute/therapeutic readings** ❓ — the interpretive dispute, stated in one clause

**How to use them:** both 2018 Q3(c) and 2021 Q4(c) ask you to justify or assess. So **derive** the distinction from the picture theory before you illustrate it; a list of sayable and showable items, unaccompanied by the derivation, is the answer that loses the assessment marks.

#### Plain-language visual — the eye that cannot see itself

```text
   WHAT A PROPOSITION DOES                WHAT IT CANNOT DO
   +-------------------------------+      +---------------------------+
   | It SAYS that things stand in  |      | It cannot SAY that it has |
   | a certain way.                |      | the form that lets it say |
   |                               |      | anything at all.          |
   | "The book is on the table."   |      |                           |
   | -> a possible arrangement of  |      | To do that it would need  |
   |    objects is presented       |      | to step OUTSIDE the form  |
   +-------------------------------+      | and look at it - i.e.     |
                                          | outside the possibility   |
                                          | of representation itself. |
                                          | THERE IS NO SUCH PLACE.   |
                                          +---------------------------+
                       |                                 |
                      v                                v
             THE ANALOGY THAT MAKES IT CLICK
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | The EYE is not part of the visual field. Nothing you see tells |
   | you that you are seeing it. The eye's presence is not a fact   |
   | IN the picture; it is a condition OF there being a picture.    |
   |                                                                |
   | Wittgenstein uses exactly this image for the metaphysical      |
   | subject at 5.632-5.633: "the subject does not belong to the    |
   | world; rather, it is a LIMIT of the world."                    |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
                       |
                       v
   AND THEN THE AWKWARD QUESTION THE BOOK ASKS ITSELF
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | Every sentence of the Tractatus is about logical form, objects |
   | and the limits of language. By its own test, NONE of them says |
   | anything. 6.54: the reader who understands them recognises     |
   | them as NONSENSICAL, and throws away the ladder.               |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
```

**In simple words:** you can photograph everything in a room except the camera taking the photograph, and no photograph can show you the fact that it is a photograph. Wittgenstein's claim is not that logical form is a very mysterious object that we can only glimpse. It is that logical form is not an object at all — it is what makes objects representable, and so there is nothing there to be photographed.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| The distinction is **derived** | it follows from the picture theory; it is not an extra doctrine |
| Logical form **shows itself** | in every meaningful proposition, and cannot be stated |
| The limit is drawn **in language** | 5.6 and the Preface; there is no outside vantage point |
| Ethics is **not false** | it is not a factual proposition at all — a different verdict entirely |
| 7 stands **alone** | ⚠️ there is no proposition 7.1; writing "7.1" is a giveaway error |

> **One-line memory:** the eye is not in the visual field.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — the sayable, the shown, and the two ways to read the ending

```text
   THE TABLE THAT ANSWERS "WHAT IS THE DISTINCTION?"
```
